Thermal 𝒏 \bm{n} -Point Conformal Blocks in Four Dimensions from Oscillator Representations
Martin Ammon, Jakob Hollweck, Tobias Hössel, Katharina Wölfl

Abstract
We define and compute the four-dimensional thermal n n -point conformal block in the projection channel using oscillator representations on 𝕊 β 1 × 𝕊 3 \mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3} . This is done by evaluating a class of integrals over the homogeneous space 𝔻 4 \mathbb{D}_{4} of the four-dimensional conformal group. We restrict ourselves to scalar external operators and scalar exchange. In the low-temperature limit, our result reduces correctly to the vacuum ( n + 2 ) (n+2) -point block in the comb channel. The corresponding expressions can be written as a series of terminating hypergeometric functions or equivalently, a series of weighted SU(2) spin-networks. Alternatively, functions adapted to the SU(2,2) representation are introduced and some properties are discussed.



1
Introduction

The central objects of any conformal field theory (CFT), namely correlation functions, are largely characterised by their decomposition into conformal blocks. The scalar
n
-point correlators of a CFT on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are fixed by their symmetry until
n=3
, up to the three-point coefficients
C_{ijk}
and the conformal weights
\Delta_{i}
. For higher-point conformal correlation functions, the operator product expansion (OPE) defines a natural split between the kinematical data, given by the conformal blocks, and the dynamical, theory-dependent data that enters as input to these blocks. Because the OPE is associative, there are consistency conditions called crossing equations between different expansions of the correlation functions. The bootstrap program constrains the dynamical data of conformal field theories through crossing symmetry and other consistency conditions, and has become a powerful tool in both analytic and numerical investigations, see e. g.
Ferrara:1973yt
;
Polyakov:1974gs
;
Rattazzi:2008pe
;
El-Showk:2012cjh
;
Hartman:2022zik
.

The crossing symmetry equations for higher-point correlators do not impose fundamentally new constraints beyond those already present in the four-point functions. Rather, constraint equations for higher-point correlation functions can be interpreted as a repackaging of (infinitely many) four-point crossing equations. This reformulation is advantageous for the conformal bootstrap, as it allows access to an infinite set of four-point constraints, something which is otherwise out of reach. Indeed, actually solving for (and not only constraining) the full CFT data of a theory is intrinsically a task that makes this approach necessary, as one needs to account for four-point functions of all operators. See
Antunes:2020yzv
;
Buric:2020zlb
;
Harris:2025cxx
for work on the multi-point conformal bootstrap. It has also been suggested in
Rosenhaus:2018zqn
that instead of studying four-point crossing equations for external spinning operators, one can consider higher-point crossing with external scalar fields.

This motivates the extension of conformal bootstrap methods to thermal settings, where finite-temperature effects must be taken into account. In
d=2
, the vacuum conformal data
\{\Delta_{i},C_{ijk}\}
is sufficient to even determine the finite-temperature (equivalently finite size with periodic boundary conditions) corrections to conformal correlation functions on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{R}
, periodic Euclidean time with circumference
\beta=\frac{1}{T}
accounting for the temperature. This is because one can map the (punctured) plane conformally to the cylinder
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{R}
. In higher dimensions, there is no such conformal map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
and hence one needs additional data, namely the one-point coefficient
b_{\mathcal{O}}
of the scalar one-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\rangle_{\beta}=b_{\mathcal{O}}T^{\Delta}

Petkou:2018ynm
. Because the thermal correlation functions on
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
do not manifestly satisfy the KMS condition in general, one gets
thermal crossing equations
which constrain
b_{\mathcal{O}}
in terms of the vacuum data
\{\Delta_{i},C_{ijk}\}
. For the two-point function, this has been studied for a wide range of settings in various dimensions, see for example
Iliesiu:2018fao
;
Iliesiu:2018zlz
;
Karlsson:2022osn
;
Alday:2020eua
;
Dodelson:2022yvn
;
Huang:2022vet
;
Dodelson:2023vrw
;
Esper:2023jeq
;
Marchetto:2023xap
;
Barrat:2024aoa
;
Buric:2025anb
;
Barrat:2025wbi
;
Barrat:2025nvu
.

Instead of working on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, one can consider
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, which is more general, as it depends on the scale
\beta/L
, with
\beta
thermal circle circumference and
L
the sphere radius. Taking the high-temperature limit
\beta\to 0
then relates correlators on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
to those on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. Thermal correlation functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
are, due to the KMS condition, effectively defined on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. In particular, the aforementioned thermal crossing equations for two-point functions can be understood, via the high-temperature limit, as arising from crossing equations for four-point functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with some external fields summed over
Iliesiu:2018fao
. This limit is, however, challenging to take in practice, one reason being its sensitivity to CFT data at large scaling dimensions. One can also argue that
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is the more natural setting for thermodynamic phenomena as it allows for the inclusion of angular potentials. Furthermore, in contrast to
\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the one-point function is not fixed by symmetry and thus becomes a non-trivial observable. See for example
Buric:2024kxo
;
Buric:2025uqt
for this perspective.

We expect higher-point conformal blocks in thermal CFTs to play a similar role as in vacuum CFTs: higher-point crossing equations related to
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
should give rise to higher-point thermal crossing equations on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
that in theory allow to determine
b_{\mathcal{O}}
in terms of
\{C_{ijk},\Delta_{i}\}
more effectively than with the thermal crossing equation arising from the four-point function. So far, however, there are to our knowledge no results on thermal conformal blocks besides one-point blocks (see e. g.
Gobeil:2018fzy
;
Buric:2024kxo
;
Alkalaev:2024jxh
) for dimensions higher than two. We address this problem by defining and computing the
n
-point conformal block on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
in the so-called projection (or necklace) channel. For the known previous results in this channel in two dimensions, see for example
Hadasz2010
;
Kraus2017
;
Cho:2017oxl
;
Alkalaev:2017bzx
;
Alkalaev:2020yvq
;
Alkalaev:2022kal
;
Alkalaev:2023evp
;
Pavlov:2023asi
.

Conformal blocks can be defined naturally through eigenvalue equations of the respective Casimir elements in addition to the conformal Ward identities. A direct approach to computing them in higher dimensions is to solve these differential equations as was first done in
Dolan:2000ut
;
Dolan:2003hv
;
Dolan:2011dv
for the four-point conformal block. More recently, this method has been generalised to blocks for
n>4
in dimensions
d>2
in
Buric:2020dyz
;
Buric:2021ywo
;
Buric:2021ttm
;
Buric:2021kgy
.

We construct the conformal blocks using the oscillator construction for four-dimensional Euclidean CFTs introduced in
Ammon:2024axd
. The blocks are implemented as gluings of wavefunctions defined on the Bergman space
\mathcal{H}L^{2}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
, where
\mathbb{D}_{4}
is a homogeneous space of the four-dimensional conformal group. This leads to a class of integrals of basis functions
\phi^{j,m}_{a,b}
over
\mathbb{D}_{4}
which we compute by a decomposition suited to the coset structure of
\mathbb{D}_{4}
and by introducing an expansion for the pointwise product of two functions
\phi^{j,m}_{a,b}
. Both the class of integrals and the expansion generalise classic results for SU(2) Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
functions, and the derived expressions involve the SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients. This has the advantage that there are already efficient tools adjusted to the numerical evaluation of spin-network diagrams for more specific Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices and, more generally, for contractions of tensor-network diagrams. We discuss this in more detail in the outlook.

The structure of the paper is as follows. We start in section
2
by defining thermal conformal blocks on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
in the projection channel. In section
3
, we review the oscillator construction for Euclidean four-dimensional CFTs and compute the vacuum
n
-point block in the comb channel. Our result involves the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients of SU(2), so we include a short discussion of the associated SU(2) spin-network diagrams in appendix
C.5
. In section
4
, the general construction of the
n
-point thermal conformal block in the projection channel is presented. We derive the one-point block in detail and explore the low-temperature limit for the general case. We finally discuss our results in section
5
and give an outlook.

Detailed computations are presented in the appendix. Appendix
A
is dedicated to the discussion of non-vanishing angular potentials and their implementation in the calculations of thermal blocks. In appendix
B
, we list properties of Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices (for both SU(2) and its holomorphic extension to general
2\times 2
matrices) and SU(2) intertwiners. A key result for the computation of thermal and vacuum conformal blocks in the main part of this paper is given in appendix
C
, where we evaluate general integrals over basis functions of
\mathcal{H}L^{2}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
. For readers who are not familiar with the oscillator construction, appendix
D
discusses the easier case of conformal blocks in the projection channel on the two-dimensional torus.

2
Definition of Four-Dimensional Thermal Blocks in Projection Channel

In this work, we focus on Euclidean correlation functions, hence a field is parametrised by
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. The fields are taken to be bosonic and scalar primary fields. The line element
\mathrm{d}s^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}
transforms as
\mathrm{d}s^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}=\mathrm{e}^{2\tau}\mathrm{d}s^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}}
under the transformation
r=\operatorname{e}^{\tau}
, where our coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
are given by
(\tau,\Omega)
and
r^{2}=x^{\mu}x_{\mu}
. Under the same transformation, the primary scalar field
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}(x)
with conformal weight
\Delta
transforms as
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}(x)=r^{-\Delta}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}}(\tau,\Omega)
; in the following we will drop the labels denoting the space unless they are needed for clarity.

The thermal
n
-point correlation function for the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
with coordinates
(\tau,\Omega)
is given by

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_1}(\tau_1,\Omega_1)\cdots\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_n}(\tau_n,\Omega_n)}_{\beta}=\text{Tr}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}(\tau_{1},\Omega_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{n}}(\tau_{n},\Omega_{n})\,q^{D}\right)

(2.1)

for
q=\mathrm{e}^{-\beta}
and where the trace runs over the space of states of the CFT. The thermal
n
-point correlator is naturally periodic in
\tau
, which can be easily seen by inserting
n
copies of
\mathbb{1}=\operatorname{e}^{-\beta D}\operatorname{e}^{\beta D}
:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_1}(\tau_1,\Omega_1)\cdots\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_n}(\tau_n,\Omega_n)}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\left[e^{\tau_{1}D}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}(0,\Omega_{1})e^{-\tau_{1}D}\cdots e^{\tau_{n}D}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{n}}(0,\Omega_{n})e^{-\tau_{n}D}e^{-\beta D}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\left[\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}(\tau_{1}+\beta,\Omega_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{n}}(\tau_{n}+\beta,\Omega_{n})e^{-\beta D}\right]

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue{\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_1}(\tau_1 + \beta,\Omega_1)\cdots\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_n}(\tau_n + \beta,\Omega_n)}_{\beta}\,.

(2.2)

Hence, the thermal
n
-point correlation function with fields of coordinates on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
is effectively defined on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Equation (
2.2
) is known as the KMS condition; imposing it for an explicit expression of the correlation function gives non-trivial so-called
thermal crossing equations
which constrain the coefficient of the one-point correlation function in terms of the vacuum CFT data
Iliesiu:2018fao
.

One has to be careful when directly translating the KMS condition into the background geometry, as it obscures how the theory at hand is quantised, i. e. how the Hamiltonian is chosen. This translation leads to vastly different behaviour: according to above, we say that the thermal one-point function of a scalar operator defined over
\mathbb{R}^{4}
through a flat slicing is actually defined over
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and has (inverse) monomial dependence in
\beta
, i. e.
\expectationvalue{ \mathcal{O}_\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}\sim\beta^{-\Delta}
. The thermal one-point function for the scalar primary
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}(x)
discussed before, however, is defined through radial quantization and has a hypergeometric dependence in
\beta

Gobeil:2018fzy
;
Buric:2024kxo
;
Alkalaev:2024jxh
.

In general, there exists a plethora of channels for the thermal
n
-point block. We focus here on the so-called
projection channel
and define it by inserting
n
projectors
1
1

1

We are restricting ourselves to scalar exchange. In the more general case including internal spinning operators, the projector would also depend on the spins
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
in addition to the scaling dimension
\mathbb{\Delta}
.

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}
into the correlation function in the following way

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,(\tau_{1},\Omega_{1}),\dots,(\tau_{n},\Omega_{n}))\equiv\text{Tr}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}}q^{D}\mathcal{O}_{1}(\tau_{1},\Omega_{1})\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{2}}(\tau_{2},\Omega_{2})\cdots\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{n}}\mathcal{O}_{n}(\tau_{n},\Omega_{n})\right).

(2.3)

We will, however, compute this object for coordinates
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and use

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,(\tau_{1},\Omega_{1}),\dots,(\tau_{n},\Omega_{n}))=r_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}\cdots r_{n}^{\Delta_{n}}G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\,

(2.4)

in the end to translate it back to
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
We want to stress that also the block parametrised by coordinates
x_{i}
is still defined on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
because it is inherently defined through radial quantization.

The projectors
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,n
project onto a representation space of
\mathfrak{su}(2,2)
by definition. We discuss that the natural choice for this space is the functional space
\mathcal{H}L^{2}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
consisting of square integrable holomorphic functions on the domain
\mathbb{D}_{4}
in section
3.1
, where all further details are provided. The projector
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}
can then be expressed via an integral over
\mathbb{D}_{4}

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\left[\operatorname{d}\!U_{i}\right]_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}\outerproduct*{U_i^\dagger}{U_i}\,

(2.5)

with coordinates
U_{i}
. Using this form of the projectors we can rewrite equation (
2.3
) as

G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\left[\operatorname{d}\!U_{1}\right]\cdots\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\left[\operatorname{d}\!U_{n}\right]\Omega(x_{1},U_{1},U_{2}^{\dagger})\Omega(x_{2},U_{2},U_{3}^{\dagger})\cdots\Omega(x_{n},U_{n},qU_{1}^{\dagger})\,,

(2.6)

for the wavefunctions

\Omega(x_{i},U_{i},U_{i+1}^{\dagger})\equiv\matrixelement*{U_i}{\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_i}(x_i)}{U_{i+1}^\dagger},

(2.7)

where cyclicity of the trace was used and the action of
q^{D}
evaluated (see additional steps in section
4
). We introduce the corresponding diagram for equation (
2.6
) as

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

(2.8)

\displaystyle=\hbox to199.97pt{\vbox to63.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.30551pt\lower-28.85275pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-20.63612pt}{4.44653pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{78.46994pt}{4.44653pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{29.1562pt}{-24.00623pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\par
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

where the integrations over
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
are implicit. The external legs at
x_{i}
carry each a weight of
\Delta_{i}
, respectively.

In
Dolan:2000ut
;
Dolan:2003hv
;
Dolan:2011dv
it was shown that the vacuum four-point conformal block is determined by the Casimir equations derived by the Casimir elements of the conformal algebra. The vacuum higher-point case was recently discussed in
Buric:2020dyz
;
Buric:2021ywo
;
Buric:2021ttm
;
Buric:2021kgy
. In the thermal case, the Casimir equation for the
d
-dimensional one-point block including angular potentials was derived in
Gobeil:2018fzy
; the same strategy can be applied to the
n
-point block in the projection channel. In general,
\mathfrak{su}(2,2)
has three Casimir operators
C_{2}
,
C_{3}
and
C_{4}
. For the scalar representation,
C_{3}
is identically zero. Furthermore, one can check that in this case
C_{4}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}=(\frac{1}{4}C_{2}^{2}+C_{2})\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}
holds as an operator identity, meaning that the scalar representation of
\mathfrak{su}(2,2)
is fully characterised by
C_{2}
.
2
2

2

Because we are focusing on scalar exchange, no additional vertex differential operators that were proposed in proposed in
Buric:2020dyz
;
Buric:2021ywo
;
Buric:2021ttm
;
Buric:2021kgy
are necessary. The Casimir operator
C_{4}
that commutes with the algebra in equation (
3.5
) is for the scalar representation given by

\displaystyle C_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(K_{\mu}K^{\mu}P_{\nu}P^{\nu}+4K_{\mu}M^{\mu\nu}M_{\nu\rho}P^{\rho}+4K_{\mu}M^{\mu\nu}P_{\nu}(D-6)+\frac{3}{4}(M_{\mu\nu}M^{\mu\nu})^{2}\right.+M_{\mu\nu}M^{\mu\nu}(D^{2}-8D+C_{2}+22)

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 8.19447pt\,-D^{4}+16D^{3}-80D^{2}+128D+36C_{2}-16C_{2}D+2C_{2}D^{2}\right)\,.

The quadratic Casimir element for
\mathfrak{su}(2,2)
is given by

\displaystyle C_{2}=D^{2}-\frac{1}{2}M_{\mu\nu}M^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{\mu}K^{\mu}+K_{\mu}P^{\mu}\right)\,.

(2.9)

Inserting
C_{2}
into the conformal block (
2.3
), commuting through and using cyclicity, one can derive the respective Casimir differential equation in the same way as it was done for the one-point block in
Gobeil:2018fzy
. As one can easily see from the derivation of the same Casimir equation in the two-dimensional case (see e. g.
Kraus2017
and appendix
D
) the difference between the one-point and the
n
-point Casimir equation in the projection channel, is the change of the differential operators
\mathcal{L}^{(x)}
to multi-point differential operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}^{(x_{i})}\,,\qquad\mathcal{L}^{(x)}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(x)=[L,\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(x)]\,,

(2.10)

in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
.
Note that one needs to introduce angular potentials
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}M_{03}}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}M_{12}}
for the derivation of any Casimir equation, even for the blocks with
\mu_{2}=\mu_{3}=0
, which poses an obstruction if one is interested only in the purely thermal case. We omit the explicit form of the Casimir equation as well as its derivation here because the method we present allows us to directly obtain the blocks at vanishing potential, without making this detour.

One can see from equation (
2.6
) that the density matrix
q^{D}
amounts to a finite transformation of the
U^{\dagger}_{1}
coordinate, effectively changing boundary conditions before gluing. The presence of additional angular potentials
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}M_{03}}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}M_{12}}
,
M_{03}
and
M_{12}
being the other two elements of the Cartan subalgebra, gives rise to additional finite transformations of
U^{\dagger}_{1}
in equation (
2.6
), leading to “twists” in the boundary conditions. These twists are then reflected in the oscillator diagram (
2.8
) by a label
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}M_{03}}\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}M_{12}}qU_{1}^{\dagger}
instead of just
qU_{1}^{\dagger}
. Computing these actions and, thus, incorporating the angular potentials in our analysis is straight forward; we give more details on that in appendix
A
.
We focus, however, on the limit of vanishing angular potentials
\mu_{2}=\mu_{3}=0
in the following. Note that even though this limit is difficult to take for the respective Casimir equation, it does not pose any problems here, as the angular potentials act finitely.

3
\bm{n}
-Point Blocks in the Comb Channel

The aim of this chapter is to compute the scalar
n
-point conformal block in the comb channel, which is the low-temperature limit of the
(n-2)
-point blocks at finite
T
in the projection channel, as will be discussed in section
4
. This calculation simultaneously generalises the results in
Ammon:2024axd
and sets the stage for the derivation of thermal higher-point blocks in the following chapter. We start by reviewing the oscillator construction that we use for the computation before moving on to the actual computation and conclude with some consistency checks concerning our result.

3.1
Review of the Oscillator Construction

Our next objective is to introduce the oscillator method with which we aim to eventually compute higher-point thermal blocks. This approach was first used in
Kraus2020
to rederive global blocks in two dimensions and the framework was generalised to four dimensions in
Ammon:2024axd
. We begin by giving a quick review of the oscillator formalism in four dimensions.

Oscillator representations are unitary irreducible representations of the conformal algebra. The four-dimensional conformal group
3
3

3

Even though we here are interested in the Euclidean CFT, we study the corresponding unitary highest-weight representations of the Lorentzian conformal group. Furthermore, we really only care about the Lie algebra, so we use the term conformal group somewhat loosely. Since the Lie algebras of SO(4,2) and SU(2,2) are isomorphic, we refer to both simply as the conformal group. See for example
Luscher:1974ez
.
is given by
\text{SO}(4,2)
. Since the conformal algebra is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{su}(2,2)
, we start our construction from the quotient space

\mathbb{D}_{4}\cong\text{SU}(2,2)/(\text{SU}(2)\times\text{SU}(2)\times\text{U}(1))\,

(3.1)

of
\text{SU}(2,2)
by its maximally compact subgroup.
The domain
\mathbb{D}_{4}
consists of
(2\times 2)
-matrices
U
such that
\mathbb{1}-UU^{\dagger}
is positive-definite, and can thus be interpreted as a higher-dimensional generalisation of the complex unit disc
\mathbb{D}
that plays an essential role in the two-dimensional oscillator formalism.

As representation space we use the weighted Bergman space
\mathcal{H}L^{2}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
, the space of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{D}_{4}
with finite norm induced by the inner product

\displaystyle(f,g)

\displaystyle=c_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\frac{\operatorname{d}\!U}{\mathrm{det}^{4-\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{1}-UU^{\dagger})}\overline{f(U)}g(U)\equiv\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\,\overline{f(U)}g(U)\,.

(3.2)

Here,
\operatorname{d}\!U
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
and the scaling dimension
\mathbb{\Delta}
labels our representations. The prefactor
c_{\mathbb{\Delta}}=\pi^{-4}(\mathbb{\Delta}-1)(\mathbb{\Delta}-2)^{2}(\mathbb{\Delta}-3)
in (
3.2
) is chosen in such a way that the constant function
1
has unit norm. We usually drop the subscript of
[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}
.

Based on this functional space, we represent the generators of conformal transformations as differential operators in terms of coordinates
w^{\mu}
parametrizing the domain
\mathbb{D}_{4}
through Pauli matrices
\sigma^{j}
and unit matrix
\sigma^{0}
as

U=w_{0}\sigma^{0}-iw_{1}\sigma^{1}-iw_{2}\sigma^{2}-iw_{3}\sigma^{3}\,,

(3.3)

where the entries of
U
are denoted by
u_{ij}
.
This allows us to represent the generators
D,P^{\mu},K^{\mu}
and
M^{\mu\nu}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{P}^{\mu}=\partial^{\mu}\,,\quad\mathfrak{K}^{\mu}=w^{2}\partial^{\mu}-2w^{\mu}w_{\rho}\partial^{\rho}-2w^{\mu}\mathbb{\Delta}\,,\\
\mathfrak{D}=w_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}+\mathbb{\Delta}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu}=w^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}-w^{\nu}\partial^{\mu}\,,\end{split}

(3.4)

where
w^{2}=w_{\nu}w^{\nu}
is the Euclidean norm squared. One can check that these operators obey the correct algebra

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{P}^{\mu},\,\mathfrak{P}^{\nu}]=0\,,\qquad

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{D},\,\mathfrak{P}^{\mu}]=-\mathfrak{P}^{\mu}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu},\,\mathfrak{P}^{\rho}]=-\delta^{\mu\rho}\mathfrak{P}^{\nu}+\delta^{\nu\rho}\mathfrak{P}^{\mu}\,,

(3.5a)

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{K}^{\mu},\,\mathfrak{K}^{\nu}]=0\,,\quad

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{D},\,\mathfrak{K}^{\mu}]=\mathfrak{K}^{\mu}\,,\quad

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu},\,\mathfrak{K}^{\rho}]=-\delta^{\mu\rho}\mathfrak{K}^{\nu}+\delta^{\nu\rho}\mathfrak{K}^{\mu}\,,

(3.5b)

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu},\,\mathfrak{D}]=0\,,

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{K}^{\mu},\,\mathfrak{P}^{\nu}]=2(\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu}+\delta^{\mu\nu}\mathfrak{D})\,,

(3.5c)

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu},\mathfrak{M}^{\rho\sigma}]=-\delta^{\mu\rho}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{\nu\sigma}-\delta^{\nu\sigma}\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\rho}+\delta^{\nu\rho}\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\sigma}+

\displaystyle\,\delta^{\mu\sigma}\mathfrak{M}^{\nu\rho}\,,

(3.5d)

as well as the adjoint relations

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{P}^{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{K}^{\mu}\,,

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{M}^{\mu\nu}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{D}

(3.6)

with respect to the inner product of the Bergman space.
The base polynomials

\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)=\text{det}^{m}(U)\mathcal{D}^{j}_{a,b}(U)

(3.7)

are a modification of the (holomorphically extended) Wigner
\mathcal{D}
-matrices
4
4

4

In this expression
k
runs from
\max(0,a+b)
to
\min(j+a,j+b)
.

\mathcal{D}^{j}_{a,b}(U)=\sqrt{\frac{(j+a)!(j-a)!}{(j+b)!(j-b)!}}\sum\limits_{k}\binom{j+b}{k}\binom{j-b}{k-a-b}u^{k}_{11}u^{j+a-k}_{12}u^{j+b-k}_{21}u^{k-a-b}_{22}\,,

(3.8)

and depend on four parameters:
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
, as well as
a,b=-j,-j+1,\dots,j
. In
Calixto2010
, it was shown that these polynomials indeed form an orthogonal basis as their inner product is given by

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}}{\phi_{a',b'}^{j',m'}}=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\overline{\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)}\,\phi_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(U)=(\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\mathbb{\Delta}})^{-2}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}\,,

(3.9)

with normalisation factor

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}=\sqrt{\frac{d_{j}}{\mathbb{\Delta}-1}\binom{m+\mathbb{\Delta}-2}{\mathbb{\Delta}-2}\binom{d_{j}+m+\mathbb{\Delta}-2}{\mathbb{\Delta}-2}}\,,\qquad d_{j}=2j+1\,.

(3.10)

Note that we could also work in the orthonormal basis
\varphi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)=\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)
, however, this makes the base polynomials
\mathbb{\Delta}
-dependent which we want to avoid. As was already pointed out in
Ammon:2024axd
, it is necessary to analyse higher order products, such as
\innerproduct{ \phi_{a_1,b_1}^{j_1,m_1}\phi_{a_2,b_2}^{j_2,m_2}}{\phi_{a_3,b_3}^{j_3,m_3}\phi_{a_4,b_4}^{j_4,m_4}}
, to compute higher-point blocks – we dedicate appendix
C
to this.

We need to introduce two more concepts of the oscillator method before turning to the higher order integrals. For weighted Bergman spaces like
\mathcal{H}L^{2}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
there exist reproducing kernels, i. e. functions
K_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(U,U^{\prime})
that are holomorphic (anti-holomorphic) in
U^{\prime}
(
U
) and obey a reproducing identity

f(U^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}K_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(U,U^{\prime})f(U)\,

(3.11)

for all
f
in
\mathcal{H}L^{2}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
.
In our case, this kernel takes a rather compact form and allows for a simple expansion in terms of the previously introduced base polynomials, namely

\begin{split}K_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(U,U^{\prime})&=\mathrm{det}^{-\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{1}-U^{\dagger}U^{\prime})\\
&=\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}/2}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{a,b=-j}^{j}(\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\mathbb{\Delta}})^{2}\overline{\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)}\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U^{\prime})\\
&\equiv\sum\limits_{a,b}^{j,m}(\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\mathbb{\Delta}})^{2}\overline{\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)}\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U^{\prime})\,\end{split}

(3.12)

as was shown in
Calixto2010
. We will repeatedly make use of this kind of expansion throughout this paper. Note that the existence of the reproducing kernel implies the existence of a resolution of identity, which is given as the projection operator
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}
.

Let us now turn towards the key ingredients for the computation of conformal blocks, the oscillator wavefunctions. Instead of working with Euclidean coordinates
x_{\mu}
we can equivalently use a complex matrix

X=x_{0}\sigma^{0}+ix_{1}\sigma^{1}+ix_{2}\sigma^{2}+ix_{3}\sigma^{3}\,.

(3.13)

expressing the coordinate in the Pauli matrix basis.
The conformal generators then act in the usual way on the fields
[L,\mathcal{O}(x)]=\mathcal{L}^{(x)}\mathcal{O}(x)
, as operators
\mathcal{L}^{(x)}
with respect to
x_{\mu}
. We take
L
as a placeholder for the conformal generators and the corresponding differential operators
\mathcal{L}^{(x)}
explicitly read

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{P}_{\mu}&=\partial_{\mu}\,,\quad\mathcal{K}_{\mu}=x^{2}\partial_{\mu}-2x_{\mu}x^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}-2x_{\mu}\Delta\,,\\
\mathcal{D}&=x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}+\Delta\,,\quad\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}=x_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}-x_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\,.\end{split}

(3.14)

The remaining (second-level) oscillator wavefunctions, in addition to the one we already introduced (
2.7
), are defined as the matrix elements

\displaystyle\chi(X_{1},X_{2};U^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\equiv\chi_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\mathbb{\Delta}}(X_{1},X_{2};U^{\dagger})=\matrixelement*{0}{\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_1}(X_1)\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_2}(X_2)}{U^\dagger}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\,,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\psi\left(X_{1},X_{2};U\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\psi_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\mathbb{\Delta}}(X_{1},X_{2};U)={}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\hskip-2.27626pt\matrixelement{U}{\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_1}(X_1)\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_2}(X_2)}{0}\,.

(3.16)

Using the conformal invariance of the vacuum state and commuting generator insertions with the primary operators, one arrives at the oscillator equations

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left(\mathfrak{L}^{(\bar{w})\phantom{\dagger}}\!\!+\mathcal{L}^{(x_{1})}+\mathcal{L}^{(x_{2})}\right)\chi(x_{1},x_{2};\bar{w})\,,

(3.17)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left(\mathfrak{L}^{(w)\dagger}-\mathcal{L}^{(x_{1})}-\mathcal{L}^{(x_{2})}\right)\psi(x_{1},x_{2};w)\,,

(3.18)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left(\mathfrak{L}^{(w_{1})\dagger}-\mathfrak{L}^{(\bar{w}_{2})}-\mathcal{L}^{(x)}\right)\Omega(x;w_{1},\bar{w}_{2})\,.

(3.19)

The solutions of these systems of PDEs read

\displaystyle\chi(X_{1},X_{2};U^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}^{-\alpha}(X_{1}-U^{\dagger})\,\mathrm{det}^{-\beta}(X_{2}-U^{\dagger})\,\mathrm{det}^{-\gamma}(X_{1}-X_{2})\,,

(3.20)

\displaystyle\psi(X_{1},X_{2};U)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}^{-\alpha}\left(\mathbb{1}-U{X_{1}}\right)\mathrm{det}^{-\beta}\left(\mathbb{1}-U{X_{2}}\right)\mathrm{det}^{-\gamma}(X_{1}-X_{2})\,,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\Omega(X;U_{1},U_{2}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}^{-\tilde{\alpha}}\left(\mathbb{1}-U_{1}X\right)\mathrm{det}^{-\tilde{\beta}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{1}{U_{2}^{\dagger}})\,\mathrm{det}^{-\tilde{\gamma}}(X-U_{2}^{\dagger})\,,

(3.22)

with sets of exponents

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{1}+\mathbb{\Delta}-\Delta_{2})\,,

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{2}+\mathbb{\Delta}-\Delta_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\mathbb{\Delta})\,

(3.23)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta+\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{2})\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{\Delta}_{2}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}-\Delta)\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta+\mathbb{\Delta}_{2}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{1})\,.

(3.24)

With this, essentially all the tools for the computation of the
n
-point comb blocks and thermal blocks are laid out.

3.2
Computing the
\bm{n}
-Point Comb Block

Let us now turn to the explicit computations of scalar conformal blocks from the oscillator formalism. In
Ammon:2024axd
, this method was already used to reproduce the results for the four-point block
Dolan:2000ut
;
Dolan:2003hv
and a special case of the
n
-point block in the comb channel. Using the solution to what we call the
(2,2)
-integral, calculated in appendix
C
, we can now approach the general case of the
n
-point comb block with external and inner scalar weights. Note that this block was first computed in
Fortin:2019zkm
;
Parikh:2019dvm
.

Starting from the correlation function of
n
scalar primary operators
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{i}}
with scaling dimensions
\Delta_{i}
, the corresponding comb block

G^{(n)}_{\text{Comb}}=\expectationvalue{\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_1}(x_1)\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_2}(x_2)\mathbb{P}_{\Deltabb_1}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_3}(x_3)\mathbb{P}_{\Deltabb_2}\dots\mathbb{P}_{\Deltabb_{n-3}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{n-1}}(x_{n-1})\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_n}(x_n)}

(3.25)

is obtained via the insertion of
(n-3)
projectors
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{j}}
. Expressing the projectors through integrals over the domain
\mathbb{D}_{4}
, the block reads

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Comb}}=

\displaystyle\int[\operatorname{d}\!U_{1}]\dots[\operatorname{d}\!U_{n-3}]\chi_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}}(X_{1},X_{2};U_{1}^{\dagger})\prod_{i=2}^{n-3}\Omega_{\Delta_{i+1},\mathbb{\Delta}_{i-1},\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}(X_{i+1};U_{i-1},U_{i}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\quad\times\psi_{\Delta_{n-1},\Delta_{n},\mathbb{\Delta}_{n-3}}(X_{n-1},X_{n};U_{n-3})

(3.26)

in terms of the oscillator wavefunctions. The corresponding diagram looks like

(3.27)

where we suppressed the integrations over
U_{1}
to
U_{n-3}
. Using the oscillator equations (
3.17
), (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), we can show that the construction (
3.26
) for the
n
-point block in the comb channel solves Casimir eigenvalue equations for
j=1,\dots,n-3
as well as the Ward identities

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(X_{1},\dots,X_{j+1})}_{2}G_{\text{Comb}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{\Delta}_{j}(\mathbb{\Delta}_{j}-4)\,G_{\text{Comb}}^{(n)}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})}G_{\text{Comb}}^{(n)}=0\,.

(3.28)

Here,
\mathcal{L}
stands for the different multipoint generators in equation (
3.14
). All one has to do is use the ansatz (
3.26
) and rewrite the differential operators acting on the wavefunctions through their respective oscillator equation. In this way, one ends up either with trivial actions
\mathfrak{C}_{2}=\mathbb{\Delta}(\mathbb{\Delta}-4)
or terms that drop out due to the adjoint relations (
3.6
).

The computation now runs as follows: We use the explicit expressions for the wavefunctions (
3.20
), (
3.21
) as well as (
3.22
) and expand each determinant in base polynomials similarly to the kernel (
3.12
). This reduces the integrals to higher-order products that we discuss in the appendix
C
. To simplify the calculation a bit, we use conformal transformations to set
x_{1}\to\infty
and
x_{n-1}=0
.
5
5

5

We compensate the divergence from the
x_{1}\to\infty
limit with an additional factor of
{\det}^{\Delta_{1}}{(X_{1})}
. The choice of setting
x_{n-1}=0
instead of
x_{n}=0
is somewhat arbitrary; the only thing that would be different in the result is the change
\Delta_{n-1}\leftrightarrow\Delta_{n}
or equivalently
\alpha_{n-2}\leftrightarrow\beta_{n-2}
.
Due to this choice there only remains one factor to expand in both
\chi
and
\psi
, so we find

\begin{split}\chi_{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}}(X_{1},X_{2};U_{1}^{\dagger})&={\det}^{-\alpha_{1}}(X_{1}-U_{1}^{\dagger})\,{\det}^{-\beta_{1}}(X_{2}-U_{1}^{\dagger})\,{\det}^{-\gamma_{1}}(X_{1}-X_{2})\\
&\!\overset{X_{1}\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}{\det}^{-\beta_{1}}(X_{2})\,{\det}^{-\beta_{1}}(\mathbb{1}-X_{2}^{-1}U_{1}^{\dagger})\\
&={\det}^{-\beta_{1}}(X_{2})\,\sum_{a_{1},b_{1}}^{j_{1},m_{1}}\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\beta_{1}}\right)^{2}\overline{\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(U_{1})}\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(X_{2}^{-1})\,,\end{split}

(3.29)

and

\begin{split}&\psi_{\Delta_{n-1},\Delta_{n},\mathbb{\Delta}_{n-3}}(X_{n-1},X_{n};U_{n-3})\\
&={\det}^{-\alpha_{n-2}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{n-3}X_{n-1})\,{\det}^{-\beta_{n-2}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{n-3}X_{n})\,{\det}^{-\gamma_{n-2}}(X_{n-1}-X_{n})\\
&\!\overset{X_{n-1}\to 0}{\longrightarrow}{\det}^{-\gamma_{n-2}}(X_{n})\,{\det}^{-\beta_{n-2}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{n-3}X_{n})\\
&={\det}^{-\gamma_{n-2}}(X_{n})\,\sum_{a_{n-2},b_{n-2}}^{j_{n-2},m_{n-2}}\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{n-2},m_{n-2}}_{\beta_{n-2}}\right)^{2}\phi_{a_{n-2},b_{n-2}}^{j_{n-2},m_{n-2}}(U_{n-3})\phi_{a_{n-2},b_{n-2}}^{j_{n-2},m_{n-2}}(X_{n}^{T})\,.\end{split}

(3.30)

Slightly more involved is the expansion of the
\Omega
-wavefunctions, since each has three factors that need to be expanded. To make our notation more compact, let us introduce some short-hand notation. First, we group the four indices of each base polynomial in one block-index such as
(i)\equiv(j_{i},m_{i},a_{i},b_{i})
. This includes writing the sum over all included indices simply as
\sum_{(i)}
and the polynomials as
\phi^{(i)}
. Moreover, if we have primed indices such as
j_{i}^{\prime}
, we indicate that in the block-index as
(i^{\prime})
.
If
j_{i}
and
m_{i}
appear as a sum, we abbreviate it with
k_{i}=j_{i}+m_{i}
.
Now, we can express the expansion of each
\Omega
-wavefunction as

\begin{split}&\Omega_{\Delta_{i+1},\mathbb{\Delta}_{i-1},\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}(X_{i+1};U_{i-1},U_{i}^{\dagger})\\
&={\det}^{-\hat{\alpha}_{i}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{i-1}X_{i+1})\,{\det}^{-\hat{\beta}_{i}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{i-1}U_{i}^{\dagger})\,{\det}^{-\hat{\gamma}_{i}}(X_{i+1}-U_{i}^{\dagger})\\
&={\det}^{-\hat{\gamma}_{i}}(X_{i+1})\sum_{(i)}\sum_{(i^{\prime})}\sum_{(i^{\prime\prime})}\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{i},m_{i}}_{\hat{\alpha}_{i}}\mathcal{N}^{j_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime}}_{\hat{\beta}_{i}}\mathcal{N}^{j_{i}^{\prime\prime},m_{i}^{\prime\prime}}_{\hat{\gamma}_{i}}\right)^{2}\\
&\qquad\times\phi^{(i)}(X_{i+1}^{T})\phi^{(i)}(U_{i-1})\phi^{(i^{\prime})}(U_{i-1})\overline{\phi^{(i^{\prime})}(U_{i})}\phi^{(i^{\prime\prime})}(X_{i+1}^{-1})\overline{\phi^{(i^{\prime\prime})}(U_{i})}\,,\end{split}

(3.31)

with
i=2,3,\dots,n-3
. The exponents in equations (
3.29
), (
3.30
) and (
3.31
) are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{1}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}-\Delta_{2}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{2}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}-\Delta_{1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{i+1}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{i-1}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{i-1}-\Delta_{i+1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{i+1}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{i-1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\alpha_{n-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{n-1}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{n-3}-\Delta_{n}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\beta_{n-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{n}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{n-3}-\Delta_{n-1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\gamma_{n-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{n-1}+\Delta_{n}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{n-3}}{2}\,.

(3.32)

Plugging in the expansions and expressing the
\mathbb{D}_{4}
-integrals as higher-order inner products of base functions, we obtain

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Comb}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

(3.33)

\displaystyle={\det}^{-\beta_{1}}(X_{2})\,\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n-3}{\det}^{-\hat{\gamma}_{i}}(X_{i+1})\right)\,{\det}^{-\gamma_{n-2}}(X_{n})\,\sum_{(1)}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n-3}\sum_{(i)}\sum_{(i^{\prime})}\sum_{(i^{\prime\prime})}\right)\sum_{(n-2)}

\displaystyle\times\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\beta_{1}}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n-3}\mathcal{N}^{j_{i},m_{i}}_{\hat{\alpha}_{i}}\mathcal{N}^{j_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime}}_{\hat{\beta}_{i}}\mathcal{N}^{j_{i}^{\prime\prime},m_{i}^{\prime\prime}}_{\hat{\gamma}_{i}}\right)\mathcal{N}^{j_{n-2},m_{n-2}}_{\beta_{n-2}}\right)^{2}\hskip-5.69046pt\phi^{(1)}(X_{2}^{-1})\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n-3}\phi^{(i)}(X_{i+1}^{T})\phi^{(i^{\prime\prime})}(X_{i+1}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle\times\phi^{(n-2)}(X_{n}^{T})\innerproduct{\phi^{(1)}}{\phi^{(2)}\cdot\phi^{(2')}}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n-4}\hskip-2.84544pt\innerproduct{\phi^{(i')}\cdot\phi^{(i'')}}{\phi^{(i+1)}\cdot\phi^{(i+1')}}\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\innerproduct{\phi^{(n-3')}\cdot\phi^{(n-3'')}}{\phi^{(n-2)}}\hskip-2.27626pt.

We calculated the above integrals, which we call the
(1,2)
-,
(2,1)
- and the
(2,2)
-integral, in appendix
C
as

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\phi^{j_1,m_1}_{a_1,b_1} \phi^{j_2,m_2}_{a_2,b_2} }{\phi^{j_3,m_3}_{a_3,b_3} \phi^{j_4,m_4}_{a_4,b_4} }

(3.34)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{p_{a},p_{b}}^{l,n}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}C^{j_{1}j_{2}l}_{a_{1},a_{2},p_{a}}C^{j_{3}j_{4}l}_{a_{3},a_{4},p_{a}}C^{j_{1}j_{2}l}_{b_{1},b_{2},p_{b}}C^{j_{3}j_{4}l}_{b_{3},b_{4},p_{b}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{3}+k_{4}}\,,

with
\mathcal{N}
-coefficients given in equation (
3.10
) and the SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients. It is convenient to group the
\mathcal{N}
-coefficients in equation (
3.33
) together with the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients, defining the weighted intertwiners
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
and base polynomials as
\varphi^{(1)}_{\alpha}(U)\equiv\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\alpha}\phi^{(1)}(U)
. Taken together, we define the
\mathcal{T}
-functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{(1),(4)}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\\
\mathbb{\Delta}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{1},X_{2}\right)\equiv\sum_{(2),(3)}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{(1),(2),(3),(4)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\varphi^{(2)}_{\beta}(X_{1})\varphi^{(3)}_{\gamma}(X_{2})\,.

(3.35)

We elaborate on this in appendix (
C.4
). The
n
-point conformal block in the comb channel can then be expressed as

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Comb}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle={\det}^{-\beta_{1}}(X_{2})\,\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n-3}{\det}^{-\hat{\gamma}_{i}}(X_{i+1})\right)\,{\det}^{-\gamma_{n-2}}(X_{n})\,\sum_{(2)}\cdots\sum_{(n-3)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathcal{T}_{0,(2)}\left(\begin{matrix}\,-\,,\beta_{1},\hat{\alpha}_{2},\hat{\beta}_{2}\\
\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{2}^{-1},X_{3}^{T}\right)\left(\prod_{i=2}^{n-4}\mathcal{T}_{(i),(i+1)}\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{i},\hat{\gamma}_{i},\hat{\alpha}_{i+1},\hat{\beta}_{i+1}\\
\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{i+1}^{-1},X_{i+2}^{T}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathcal{T}_{(n-3),0}\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{n-3},\hat{\gamma}_{n-3},\beta_{n-2},\,-\,\\
\mathbb{\Delta}_{n-3}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{n-2}^{-1},X_{n}^{T}\right)\,.

(3.36)

Since the
n
-point block is a series of weighted SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients, we can use SU(2) graphical notation to visualise equation (
3.36
) as
6
6

6

We always use equation (
B.19
) to rewrite
\widetilde{I}
-tensors into
I
-tensors, because these are more standard, giving additional
d_{j}
-factors for every
\mathcal{T}
-function. This is why the
\mathcal{T}
-functions depend on matrices
X_{i}^{T}
, but the diagrams on
X^{\dagger}_{i}
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{(n)}_{\text{Comb}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\quad=\quad\hbox to249.11pt{\vbox to134.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.79373pt\lower-67.1605pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{210.19737pt}{25.66818pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(3.37)

Whereas usual SU(2) spin-networks have spin labels at every line, the labels in the diagram (
3.37
) are combinations of conformal weights and stand each for a whole block-index
(j,m,a,b)
, which are contracted. The vertices are labelled by the Kronecker deltas in the definition of the
\mathcal{T}
-function. For additional details see appendix
C.5
.

We can verify that the
n
-point block correctly reduces to an
(n-1)
-point block if one sets one of the external weights
\Delta_{i+1}
to zero and adjusts the neighbouring projectors accordingly, i. e. sets
\mathbb{\Delta}_{i-1}=\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}
, which manifests as
\hat{\alpha}_{i}=\hat{\gamma}_{i}=0
and
\hat{\beta}_{i}=\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}
in terms of the wavefunction exponents. Since
\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{0}=\delta_{m,0}\delta_{j,0}
, the restriction of the scaling dimensions enforces

\sum_{(i)}\mathcal{T}_{(i-1),(i)}\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{i-1},\hat{\gamma}_{i-1},0,\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}\\
\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{i}^{-1},X_{i+1}^{T}\right)\mathcal{T}_{(i),(i+1)}\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbb{\Delta}_{i},0,\hat{\alpha}_{i+1},\hat{\beta}_{i+1}\\
\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{i+1}^{-1},X_{i+2}^{T}\right)\\
=\mathcal{T}_{(i-1),(i+1)}\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{i-1},\hat{\gamma}_{i-1},\hat{\alpha}_{i+1},\hat{\beta}_{i+1}\\
\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{i}^{-1},X_{i+2}^{T}\right)\,.

(3.38)

In this way, the
n
-point comb block clearly reduces to the
(n-1)
-point block

\evaluated{G_{\Deltabb_1,\dots,\Deltabb_{n-3}}^{\Delta_1,\dots,\Delta_n}}_{\Delta_{i+1} = 0,\ \Deltabb_{i-1} = \Deltabb_{i}}=G_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{\Delta}_{i-2},\mathbb{\Delta}_{i},\dots,\mathbb{\Delta}_{n-3}}^{\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{i},\Delta_{i+2},\dots,\Delta_{n}}\,,

(3.39)

as it should.

4
Thermal
\bm{n}
-Point Blocks in the Projection Channel

We have already defined the thermal
n
-point projection channel block on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
in section
2
. In this section, we give explicit expressions for said conformal blocks, using the
(2,2)
-integral computed in appendix
C
, and discuss the low-temperature limit that results in the comb channel blocks from section
3
.

4.1
General Construction

The
n
-point thermal block in the projection channel is defined as

G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\text{Tr}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}}q^{D}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}}(X_{1})\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{2}}(X_{2})\cdots\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{n}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{n}}(X_{n})\right)\,.

(4.1)

Evaluating this object is easiest demonstrated in the one-point case

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Tr}\left[\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}(x)q^{D}\right]&=\sum_{n}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\mathrm{d}U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\innerproduct*{ n}{ U^\dagger}\matrixelement*{U}{\mathcal{O}_\Delta q^D}{n}\\
&=\sum_{n}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\mathrm{d}U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\matrixelement*{U}{\mathcal{O}_\Delta q^D}{n}\,\innerproduct*{n}{ U^\dagger}\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\mathrm{d}U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\matrixelement*{U}{\mathcal{O}_\Delta q^D}{U^\dagger}\,.\end{split}

(4.2)

The remaining matrix element can then be evaluated as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\matrixelement*{U}{\mathcal{O}_\Delta\operatorname{e}^{-\beta D}}{U^\dagger}&=\operatorname{e}^{-\beta\mathfrak{D}(\overline{w})}\Omega(x,U,U^{\dagger})\\
&=q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}\,\Omega(x,U,qU^{\dagger})\,,\end{split}

(4.3)

where we used in the last step that
\mathfrak{D}(\bar{w}^{\prime})=\bar{w}_{\mu}\partial_{\bar{w}}+\mathbb{\Delta}
and that hence each coordinate
\bar{w}_{\mu}
is scaled separately.
7
7

7

That one scales here only
U^{\dagger}
and not
U
can be also seen from consistency with the general
n
-point block case.
Thus, we arrive at the expression

G^{(1)}(q)=q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\Omega(x,U,qU^{\dagger})

(4.4)

for the one-point block.
Coming back to equation (
4.1
), the more general computation shows that the
n
-point projection channel block on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
can be computed by

G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}=q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\left[\operatorname{d}\!U_{1}\right]\cdots\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\left[\operatorname{d}\!U_{n}\right]\Omega(X_{1},U_{1},U_{2}^{\dagger})\Omega(X_{2},U_{2},U_{3}^{\dagger})\cdots\Omega(X_{n},U_{n},qU_{1}^{\dagger})\,.

(4.5)

We will compute the block on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
in
X_{i}
-coordinates and add the corresponding Weyl factors
r^{\Delta_{i}}
in the end.

4.2
One-Point Block

We have derived in the last section that the one-point block on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
can be computed through equation (
4.4
). Note that the single projector insertion in the one-point correlator means we equate the oscillator variables
U_{1}=U_{2}
as well as the inner weights
\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}=\mathbb{\Delta}_{2}
giving us the exponents
\tilde{\alpha}=\Delta/2
,
\tilde{\beta}=\mathbb{\Delta}-\Delta/2
and
\tilde{\gamma}=\Delta/2
in the
\Omega
-wavefunction. The computation is then analogous to the vacuum case before, with the addition of using homogeneity of the base functions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi^{j,m}_{q_{a},q_{b}}(qX)&=\text{det}^{m}(qX)\mathcal{D}^{j}_{q_{a},q_{b}}(qX)\\
&=q^{2(j+m)}\phi^{j,m}_{q_{a},q_{b}}(X)\end{split}

(4.6)

to pull out
q
from the basis. Expanding then every determinant in
\Omega
from equation (
3.22
) gives us

\displaystyle G^{(1)}(q)

\displaystyle=q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}\text{det}^{-\Delta/2}(X_{1})\sum^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}\sum^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}\sum^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\Delta/2}\mathcal{N}^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{\Delta/2}\mathcal{N}^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{\mathbb{\Delta}-\Delta/2}\right)^{2}\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(X_{1}^{T})\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(X_{1}^{-1})

\displaystyle\quad\ q^{2(k_{2}+k_{3})}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\mathrm{d}U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\overline{\phi^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}(U)\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(U)}\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(U)\phi^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}(U)\,.

(4.7)

We evaluated the general
(2,2)
-integral in the appendix according to (
C.25
) and use that
\mathrm{det}^{-\Delta/2}(X_{1})=r_{1}^{-\Delta}
. One gets in terms of the
\mathcal{T}
-function introduced in section (
C.4
)

\displaystyle G^{(1)}(q)=\frac{q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}}{r_{1}^{\Delta}}\sum_{(3)}\mathcal{T}_{(3),(3)}\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbb{\Delta}-\frac{\Delta}{2},\frac{\Delta}{2},\frac{\Delta}{2},\mathbb{\Delta}-\frac{\Delta}{2}\\
\mathbb{\Delta}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|qX^{-1}_{1},X^{T}_{1}\right)q^{2(j_{3}+m_{3})}\,,

(4.8)

for
(3)=(j_{3},m_{3},a_{3},b_{3})
. In this special case of the one-point block, we can simplify the
\mathcal{T}
-function drastically. Following equation (
C.26
), we can write this particular
(2,2)
-integral as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\innerproduct{\phi^{j_3,m_3}_{a_3,b_3} \phi^{j_2,m_2}_{a_2,b_2}}{\phi^{j_1,m_1}_{a_1,b_1} \phi^{j_3,m_3}_{a_3,b_3}}\\
&=\sum_{l}\sum_{p_{a},p_{b}}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,k_{2}+k_{3}-l}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}C^{j_{3}j_{1}l}_{a_{3},a_{1},p_{a}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}l}_{a_{3},a_{2},p_{a}}C^{j_{3}j_{1}l}_{b_{3},b_{1},p_{b}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}l}_{b_{3},b_{2},p_{b}}\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}\,.\end{split}

(4.9)

Note that this includes the implicit Kronecker-deltas
\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}
and
\delta_{b_{1},b_{2}}
. We can use orthogonality (
B.7
) to collapse the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{3},p_{a}}C^{j_{3}j_{1}l}_{a_{3},a_{1},p_{a}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}l}_{a_{3},a_{2},p_{a}}=d_{l}d_{j_{2}}^{-1}\delta_{j_{1},j_{2}}\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\,,\qquad\sum_{b_{3},p_{b}}C^{j_{3}j_{1}l}_{b_{3},b_{1},p_{b}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}l}_{b_{3},b_{2},p_{b}}=d_{l}d_{j_{2}}^{-1}\delta_{j_{1},j_{2}}\delta_{b_{1},b_{2}}\,,

(4.10)

which allows us to to split
\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}}
into
\delta_{j_{1},j_{2}}
and
\delta_{m_{1},m_{2}}
. We can subsequently evaluate the coordinate dependent part

\sum_{a_{2},b_{2}}\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(X_{1}^{T})\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(X_{1}^{-1})=d_{j_{2}}\,.

(4.11)

Finally, we have to add a factor of
r_{1}^{\Delta}
to obtain the result on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Altogether, the result is

\displaystyle G^{(1)}(q)=q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}\sum_{m_{2},m_{3}}^{j_{2},j_{3}}a^{j_{2},j_{3}}_{m_{2},m_{3}}q^{2(j_{2}+j_{3}+m_{2}+m_{3})}

(4.12)

with

a^{j_{2},j_{3}}_{m_{2},m_{3}}=d_{j_{2}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{\Delta/2}\right)^{4}\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{\mathbb{\Delta}-\Delta/2}\right)^{2}\sum_{l}(d_{l})^{2}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,j_{2}+m_{2}+j_{3}+m_{3}-l}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}\,,

(4.13)

where
l
runs from
|j_{2}-j_{3}|
to
j_{2}+j_{3}
. We have checked up to
\mathcal{O}(q^{25})
that this matches the result in
Gobeil:2018fzy
;
Buric:2024kxo
. The calculation above, which shows that the one-point block is coordinate independent, can be conveniently expressed through diagrammatic rules close
8
8

8

One uses here that cutting a line in a SU(2) diagram that separates it into two disconnected parts inserts
\sim\delta_{j_{2},j_{3}}
.
to the known rules in the SU(2) case:

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(4.14)

The assignment of weights works as in the vacuum case, with the additional information that the thermal loops and the vertical lines of
X^{-1}
get an extra factor of
q^{k_{i}}
, respectively. Note that the assignment rules (see appendix
C.5
) are valid also for the other diagrams in equation (
4.14
) if we assign the bubble diagram a weight of
d_{j}\left(\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\Delta/2}\right)^{2}
.

The limit
\Delta=0
restricts the one-point block to the “zero-point block”
\text{Tr}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}q^{D})
. It is supposed to compute the same trace as the one in the character formula of the four-dimensional conformal algebra (see e. g.
Dolan:2005wy
)

G^{\Delta=0}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(q)=\frac{q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}}{(1-q)^{4}}

(4.15)

and we can check order-by-order that (
4.12
) satisfies this. That it also has the right low-temperature behaviour for
q\to 0
can be easily seen by setting
k_{2}=k_{3}=0
in equations (
4.12
) and (
4.13
).

4.3
Computing the Projection Channel
\bm{n}
-Point Block

The
n
-point block is then a straight-forward generalisation of the one-point calculation. Using the definition (
4.5
) (or equivalently the diagram (
2.8
)), we expand wavefunctions
\Omega
of the form

\Omega(X_{i};U_{i},U_{i+1}^{\dagger})=\operatorname{det}^{-\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{i}X_{i})\,\text{det}^{-\tilde{\beta}_{i}}(\mathbb{1}-U_{i}{U_{i+1}^{\dagger}})\,\text{det}^{-\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}(X_{i}-U_{i+1}^{\dagger})

(4.16)

in the basis. The weights
\alpha_{i}
,
\beta_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}
are given as

\tilde{\alpha}_{i}=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{i}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{i+1})\,,\quad\tilde{\beta}_{i}=\frac{1}{2}(-\Delta_{i}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{i+1})\,,\quad\tilde{\gamma}_{i}=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{i}-\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}+\mathbb{\Delta}_{i+1})\,.

(4.17)

After integrating out the
U
-variables using the inner product (
3.9
), adding the Weyl factors
r_{i}^{\Delta_{i}}
and using
\operatorname{det}^{-\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}(X_{i})=r_{i}^{-2\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}
, the result is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\\
&=q^{\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{\Delta_{i}-2\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}\right)\sum_{(1),\dots,(n)}q^{2(j_{n}+m_{n})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{T}_{(i-1),(i)}\left(\begin{matrix}\tilde{\beta}_{i-1},\tilde{\gamma}_{i-1},\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\tilde{\beta}_{i}\\
\mathbb{\Delta}_{i}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X^{-1}_{i-1},X_{i}^{T}\right)\end{split}

(4.18)

which in the case of
i=1
,
i-1
evaluates as
n
and
X^{-1}_{i-1}
as
qX^{-1}_{n}
. If we again consider the block as a series of weighted SU(2) spin network diagrams, one gets

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\hbox to244.27pt{\vbox to140.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 86.28534pt\lower 37.11903pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(4.19)

where again summation is implicit.

The low-temperature limit
q\to 0
is supposed to give the vacuum blocks we have discussed in section
3.2
. We see from equation (
4.18
) that this means setting the corresponding
j_{i}
and
m_{i}
in
\mathcal{T}_{(n),(1)}
to zero. Using equation (
C.4
), this sets the
\mathcal{T}_{(n),(1)}
-function to
1
, while also producing a zero in the adjacent
\mathcal{T}
-functions
\mathcal{T}_{(0),(2)}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(n-1),(0)}
. This is easiest understood with an example. The two-point block is according to (
4.18
) and the definition of
\mathcal{T}
-functions in equation (
C.31
), disregarding
q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}
and Weyl factors
r_{1}^{\Delta_{1}}r_{2}^{\Delta_{2}}
, given by

\displaystyle G^{(2)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,X_{1},X_{2})

(4.20)

\displaystyle\sim r_{1}^{-2\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}r_{2}^{-2\tilde{\gamma}_{2}}\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{(1),\dots,(6)}q^{2(k_{2}+k_{3})}\hskip-2.84544pt\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{(2),(3),(4),(1)}^{\tilde{\beta_{2}}\tilde{\gamma}_{2}\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\tilde{\beta}_{1}}\varphi^{(3)}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{2}}(X_{2}^{-1})\varphi^{(4)}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}(X_{1}^{T})\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{(1),(5),(6),(2)}^{\tilde{\beta_{1}}\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\tilde{\beta}_{2}}\varphi^{(5)}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}(X_{1}^{-1})\varphi^{(6)}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}(X_{2}^{T})\,.

Using equation (
C.4
), the
q\to 0
limit cuts the corresponding diagram (
2.8
) at the
\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}
-line and reduces it to the corresponding four-point diagram in (
3.27
). Equation (
4.20
) reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\evaluated{G^{(2)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,X_1,X_2)}_{q \to 0}&\sim r_{1}^{-2\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}r_{2}^{-2\tilde{\gamma}_{2}}\sum_{(5),(6)}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,(5),(6),0}^{\,-\,\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\,-}\varphi^{(5)}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}(X_{1}^{-1})\varphi^{(6)}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}(X_{2}^{T})\\
&=r_{1}^{-2\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}r_{2}^{-2\tilde{\gamma}_{2}}\sum_{(5)}\frac{\mathcal{N}^{(5)}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}\mathcal{N}^{(5)}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}}{\left(\mathcal{N}^{(5)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{2}}\varphi^{(5)}_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1}}(X_{1}^{-1})\varphi^{(5)}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}(X_{2}^{T})\,.\end{split}

(4.21)

With weights defined in (
4.17
) and after the appropriate renaming of points and weights, equation (
4.21
) is exactly the vacuum four-point block with
X_{1}\to\infty
and
X_{4}\to 0
, see
Ammon:2024axd
. In terms of tensor contractions, the
q\to 0
limit is easily visualised. The reduction from the two-point block in the projection channel to the vacuum four-point block from above is given by
