(4.22)

where we dashed the lines that are deleted by the low-temperature limit and suppressed the labels. The corners of the sub-diagram on the left-hand side are naturally smoothed out because of the associated Kronecker deltas we discussed in section
C.5
. The general
n
-point block works analogously, the left-most boxes are deleted because
q\to 0
removes (i. a.)
\mathcal{T}_{(n),(1)}
in equation (
4.18
).

5
Discussion and Outlook

We defined and computed the thermal
n
-point conformal blocks in the projection channel in four Euclidean dimensions. To our knowledge, this is a new result for
n\geq 2
. We checked that their low-temperature limit is given by the corresponding vacuum blocks. This involved the computation of general integrals of the basis functions
\phi^{j,m}_{a,b}
of
\mathcal{H}L^{2}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
in appendix
C
. The generalisation to non-vanishing angular potentials is presented in appendix
A
.

Admittedly, our results for both vacuum and thermal
n
-point conformal blocks in (
3.36
) and (
4.18
) appear intricate, but they have the advantage that their basic constituent is the SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficient
C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}
. There is a vast literature discussing the properties of these coefficients; we provided a small subset in appendix
B
and emphasised throughout the text how the appearance of said coefficients made some calculations almost trivial. For example, the computation of the thermal one-point block on
\mathbb{S}^{1}_{\beta}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
in section
4.2
boils down to column switches (
B.8
) and the orthogonality relation (
B.7
); the low-temperature limit discussed in section
4.3
is just evaluating trivial cases of
C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}
according to equation (
B.9
).

We have presented the vacuum and thermal
n
-point conformal blocks as weighted series of SU(2) spin network contractions. The SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients that define this representation can be expressed in terms of terminating
{}_{3}F_{2}
-hypergeometric functions, i. e. as finite sums (see equation (
B.10
)). This structure suggests that established numerical methods for spin network contractions may be adaptable to our setting. In particular, recent advances in the numerical evaluation of contractions between four-valent SU(2) intertwiners and Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
functions with SU(2) and
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
(originally developed in the spin foam literature) could provide efficient tools for evaluating expressions of the type found in this work
Gozzini:2021kbt
;
Dona:2018nev
. These implementations, written in
C
with more recent interfaces in
Julia
, leverage high-performance computing techniques; see
Dona:2022dxs
for a pedagogical introduction. Although these packages are tailored to spin-foam amplitudes, it may be possible to adapt them to the contractions involving Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices of a general
\text{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
argument as defined in equation (
3.8
). More generally,
Julia
packages such as
TensorOperations.jl

TensorOperationsjl
offer powerful frameworks for contracting general tensor networks. Using such tools, one could define the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices from equation (
3.8
) and evaluate the finite amplitudes, i. e. the contraction over magnetic indices, in equations (
3.36
) and (
4.18
). The full conformal blocks then follow as weighted sums over these amplitudes. From this perspective, the vacuum and thermal conformal blocks constructed here can be viewed as generalisations of spin foams.

From the aforementioned SU(2) perspective, the conformal blocks can be expanded in an infinite sum of spin-network contractions that themselves are finite sums. Alternatively, one can seek functions that are intrinsically associated with the full SU(2,2) symmetry. We define the
\mathcal{T}
-functions in equation (
C.31
), which are by definition infinite sums
9
9

9

The coefficients
\mathcal{C}_{(3),(2),(1)}
in equation (
C.27
) are finite because the SU(2) CGC are, according to equation (
B.10
). Every further block index contraction in the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{T}
then adds two infinite and two finite sums.
but which give us the compact expression (
4.18
) that resembles the two dimensional result. There, the
n
-point projection channel block can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,w_{1},\dots,w_{n})=q^{\mathbb{h}_{1}-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{s_{1},\dots s_{n}=0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i,i+1}^{-\mathbb{h}_{i+1}}x_{i,i+1}^{-s_{i+1}}\frac{\tau_{s_{i},s_{i+1}}(\mathbb{h}_{i},h_{i},\mathbb{h}_{i+1})}{s_{i}!(2\mathbb{h}_{i})_{s_{i}}}q^{s_{1}}\,,

(5.1)

as we showed in appendix
D
and the resulting comb channel block in the
q\to 0
limit has the same form as in four dimensions as well. Because one can directly relate the defining coefficients
\tau_{m,n}
to the SU(1,1) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients and the
\Omega
-wavefunction to its generating function, we expect that the same holds for
\mathcal{T}_{(M),(N)}
and the four-dimensional
\Omega
-wavefunction in equation (
3.22
) with respect to the four-dimensional conformal group SU(2,2).

In
Alkalaev:2020yvq
, the computation of toroidal geodesic Witten diagrams in thermal
\text{AdS}_{3}
yielded expressions for torus conformal blocks on the boundary, expressed through Clebsch-Gordan coefficients for finite dimensional SU(1,1) representations. It would be interesting to first generalise this to unitary representations of SU(1,1) and second, to do the same for
\text{AdS}_{5}
, maybe similarly to the
d
-dimensional computation of
Gobeil:2018fzy
for the one-point block.

We have not yet discussed the high-temperature limit of the
n
-point block, i. e. its reduction to
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In two dimensions, the rich theory of the
{}_{2}F_{1}
-hypergeometric function enables one to take the
q\to 1
limit for the one-point block. However, already the higher-point blocks in the projection channel are only known in the form of equation (
5.1
) for which this is not possible anymore. In four dimensions, we are in a similar situation in the case of the one-point block, where the closed form in terms of a generalised
{}_{3}F_{2}
-hypergeometric function allows to take the
q\to 1
limit
Gobeil:2018fzy
;
Buric:2024kxo
(similarly in
Alkalaev:2024jxh
in a different form). Because our result (
4.18
) resembles for higher points the two-dimensional one in equation (
5.1
), it turns out to be harder to do anything similar. The connection formula used in
Perlmutter:2015iya
for the one-point block in two dimensions has the effect of transforming
q\to 1-q
in the argument of the hypergeometric function. A possible future direction from here is to attempt a similar transformation for (
4.18
) by using one of the various generating functions for the SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients; see, for example,
varsh1988
.

In the two-dimensional case, there is by now ample discussion on different channels of global conformal blocks, see for example
Cho:2017oxl
;
Fortin:2020bfq
;
Fortin:2020yjz
. We tried to show that in four dimensions, the computation of thermal conformal blocks in the projection channel is straightforward once the corresponding integrals over
\mathbb{D}_{4}
are computed, and hope that the same applies to blocks in different channels, as well as different topologies of spaces, see e. g.
Benjamin:2023qsc
;
Shaghoulian:2015kta
;
Belin:2016yll
. Finally, we have so far restricted ourselves so far to the scalar exchange between external scalar operators. However, general conformal blocks with external scalar operators principally involve also more general spinning representations in the exchange. We plan to address this in future work.

Appendix A
Generalisation to Non-Vanishing Angular Potentials

For the more general case of non-vanishing angular potentials, additional factors of
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}M_{03}}
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}M_{12}}
are present in the definition of the
n
-point projection channel block

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,\mu_{2},\mu_{3})\equiv\text{Tr}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{1}}q^{D}\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}M_{03}}\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}M_{12}}\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{2}}\cdots\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{\Delta}_{n}}\mathcal{O}_{n}\right).

(A.1)

We can pull the exponentials out in the same way as in the the purely thermal case in equation (
4.3
), giving in the case of the one-point block

\displaystyle G^{(1)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,\mu_{2},\mu_{3})=q^{\mathbb{\Delta}}\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\mathrm{d}U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}\mathfrak{M}_{03}}\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}\mathfrak{M}_{12}}\,\Omega(x,U,qU^{\dagger})\,.

(A.2)

Because one can show that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{03}\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)

\displaystyle=-i(a+b)\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{12}\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)

\displaystyle=-i(a-b)\phi_{a,b}^{j,m}(U)\,,

(A.3)

the action of these transformations can be evaluated explicitly. In the case of the one-point block in equation (
4.7
), the expansion of
\Omega
gives base functions
\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(U)
and
\phi^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}(U)
and the action of
\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}M_{03}}\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}M_{12}}
on those evaluates as

\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{2}M_{03}}\mathrm{e}^{\mu_{3}M_{12}}\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}\phi^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}=\mathrm{e}^{-i\mu_{2}(a_{2}+a_{3}+b_{2}+b_{3})}\mathrm{e}^{-i\mu_{3}(a_{2}+a_{3}-b_{2}-b_{3})}\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}\phi^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}

(A.4)

which can be shown by using equation (
C.24
). The coordinate independence of the one-point block at vanishing angular potential followed from equation (
4.11
). The presence of the phase from equation (
A.4
) makes it difficult to evaluate (
4.11
) as before, consistent with the fact that the presence of angular potentials should make the one-point block not translationally invariant. Note that, as in the purely thermal case, the transformations in equation (
A.2
) act on only one
\Omega
-wavefunction, even in the
n
-point block. As a result, the general case (
A.1
) works exactly in the same way.

Appendix B
Auxiliary Results on Clebsch-Gordan Coefficients and Representations

We give identities for both the SU(2) Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices and their analytic extensions. For SU(2), we roughly follow the classic reference
varsh1988
where not stated otherwise.

B.1
Identities for Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
Matrices and Clebsch-Gordan Coefficients

We introduced in equation (
3.8
) the Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrix for an arbitrary
2\times 2
complex matrix
X
. Two important properties are

\sum_{c=-j}^{j}\mathcal{D}^{j}_{a,c}(X)\mathcal{D}^{j}_{c,b}(Y)=\mathcal{D}^{j}_{a,b}(XY)\,,\qquad\mathcal{D}^{j}_{a,b}(X)=\mathcal{D}^{j}_{b,a}(X^{T})\,,

(B.1)

where
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}/2
and
a,b=-j,-j+1,\dots,j-1,j
. For
X\in\text{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, one can show
10
10

10

For example by generalising the proof in
ruehl_1970
[Ch.2.4] for
a\in\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
to
X=\sqrt{\det(X)}a\in\text{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{j}_{a,b}((X^{-1})^{T})=(-1)^{a-b}\operatorname{det}^{-2j}(X)\mathcal{D}^{j}_{-a,-b}(X)\,.

(B.2)

In the case where
X\in\text{SU}(2)
, equation (
B.2
) leads to the standard statement about complex conjugation

\mathcal{D}^{j}_{a,b}(X)=(-1)^{a-b}\overline{\mathcal{D}^{j}_{-a,-b}(X)}\,.

(B.3)

Note that one can check using (
B.2
) that (
B.3
) also holds for
X
defined in equation (
3.13
), i. e.
X=x_{0}\sigma^{0}+ix_{j}\sigma^{j}
with
x_{\mu}
real. A possibility to introduce the SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients
C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1}a_{2}c}
is then through the expansion of the product of two Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices as

\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(X)\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(X)=\sum_{j}\sum_{c_{1}c_{2}}C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1},a_{2},c_{1}}C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{b_{1},b_{2},c_{2}}\mathcal{D}^{j}_{c_{1},c_{2}}(X)\,,

(B.4)

where the coefficients vanish unless the conditions

|j_{1}-j_{2}|\leq j\leq j_{1}+j_{2}\,,\qquad c_{1}=a_{1}+a_{2}\quad(c_{2}=b_{1}+b_{2})

(B.5)

are fulfilled. This means that both the
c_{1}
and the
c_{2}
sum in equation (
B.4
) are formal in the sense that only one summand is non-zero. The Clebsch-Gordan coefficients satisfy two orthogonality conditions

\displaystyle\sum_{j=|j_{1}-j_{2}|}^{j_{1}+j_{2}}\sum_{c=-j}^{j}C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{b_{1},b_{2},c}

\displaystyle=\delta_{a_{1},b_{1}}\delta_{a_{2},b_{2}}\,,

(B.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{1}=-j_{1}}^{j_{1}}\sum_{a_{2}=-j_{2}}^{j_{2}}C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}C^{j_{1}j_{2}j^{\prime}}_{a_{1},a_{2},c^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\delta_{c,c^{\prime}}\,,

(B.7)

meaning that we choose the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients to be real. Switching columns has the following effect

\displaystyle C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j_{2}+a_{2}}\sqrt{d_{j}d_{j_{1}}^{-1}}C^{jj_{2}j_{1}}_{-c,a_{2},-a_{1}}=(-1)^{j_{2}+a_{2}}\sqrt{d_{j}d_{j_{1}}^{-1}}C^{j_{2}jj_{1}}_{-a_{2},c,a_{1}}

(B.8a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j_{1}-a_{1}}\sqrt{d_{j}d_{j_{2}}^{-1}}C^{j_{1}jj_{2}}_{a_{1},-c,-a_{2}}=(-1)^{j_{1}-a_{1}}\sqrt{d_{j}d_{j_{2}}^{-1}}C^{jj_{1}j_{2}}_{c,-a_{1},a_{2}}

(B.8b)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-j}C^{j_{2}j_{1}j}_{a_{2},a_{1},c}=(-1)^{j_{1}+j_{2}-j}C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{-a_{1},-a_{2},-c}\,,

(B.8c)

where the last equation is a symmetry and we used the notation
d_{j}=2j+1
. Finally, an important special case of
C^{jj_{1}j_{2}}_{c,a_{1},a_{2}}
is given by

C^{0j_{1}j_{2}}_{0,a_{1},a_{2}}=\delta_{j_{1},j_{2}}\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\,.

(B.9)

Note that a Clebsch-Gordan coefficient can be represented as a terminating
{}_{3}F_{2}
-hypergeometric function of unit argument , and as such, a finite sum. We give as an example one of the possible expressions as

\displaystyle C^{j_{1}j_{2}j}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}

\displaystyle=\delta_{c,a_{1}+a_{2}}\frac{\Delta(j_{1}j_{2}j)}{(j_{1}+j_{2}-j)!(-j_{2}+j+a_{1})!(-j_{1}+j-a_{2})!}

(B.10)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{d_{j}!(j_{1}+a_{1})!(j_{2}-a_{2})!(j+c)!(j-c)!}{(j_{1}-a_{1})!(j_{2}+a_{2})!}}{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}-j_{1}-j_{2}+j,\,-j_{1}+a_{1},\,-j_{2}-a_{2}\\
1-j_{1}+j-a_{2}\,,1-j_{2}+j+a_{1}\end{matrix}\Bigg|1\right]

for

\Delta(j_{1}j_{2}j)=\sqrt{\frac{(j_{1}+j_{2}-j)!(j_{1}-j_{2}+j)!(-j_{1}+j_{2}+j)!}{(1+j_{1}+j_{2}+j)!}}\,.

(B.11)

B.2
Intertwiners and Integration over SU(2)

In the case where the Wigner
D
-matrices depend solely on an element of SU(2), they define an irreducible representation of the group. They fulfil the following orthogonality relation when integrated over them

\int_{\text{SU}(2)}\operatorname{d}\!g\,\overline{\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(g)}\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(g)=d_{j_{1}}^{-1}\delta_{j_{1},j_{2}}\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\delta_{b_{1},b_{2}}

(B.12)

where
\operatorname{d}\!g
is the Haar measure
11
11

11

There is some nuance in choosing the correct numerical prefactor in
\operatorname{d}\!g
when integrating over SU(2), see
varsh1988
. However, since both the conformal blocks and the oscillator wavefunctions used are only determined up to an overall constant, we ignore this factor in the following.
of SU(2)
Makinen:2019rou
. This can be generalised to

\int_{\text{SU}(2)}\operatorname{d}\!g\,\overline{\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(g)}\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(g)\mathcal{D}^{j_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}(g)=d_{j_{1}}^{-1}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{b_{3},b_{2},b_{1}}\,.

(B.13)

Every further addition of a Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrix to the integrand of equation (
B.13
) can be absorbed by expanding the product of two matrices in terms of a third, using equation (
B.4
). This leads to the general formula

\int_{\text{SU}(2)}\operatorname{d}\!g\,\overline{\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(g)}\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(g)\cdots\mathcal{D}^{j_{N-1}}_{a_{N-1},b_{N-1}}(g)=\sum_{l_{1}\dots l_{N-3}}I^{l_{1}\dots l_{N-3}}_{a_{N},\cdots,a_{1}}I^{l_{1}\dots l_{N-3}}_{b_{N},\cdots,b_{1}}\,,

(B.14)

where the
N
-valent intertwiner
I^{l_{1},\dots,l_{N-3}}_{a_{N},\cdots,a_{1}}
is given by

I^{l_{1}\dots l_{N-3}}_{a_{N},\cdots,a_{1}}=\sum_{p_{1},\dots,p_{N-3}}C^{j_{N}j_{N-1}l_{1}}_{a_{N},a_{N-1},p_{1}}\left(\prod_{i=2}^{N-3}C^{l_{i-1}j_{N-i}l_{i}}_{p_{i-1}a_{N-i}p_{i}}\right)C^{l_{N-3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{p_{N-3},a_{2},a_{1}}\,.

(B.15)

The
p_{i}
run between
-l_{i}
and
l_{i}
and the
l_{i}
as

\displaystyle|j_{N}-j_{N-1}|\leq l_{1}\leq j_{N}+j_{N-1}\,,

\displaystyle|l_{1}-j_{N-2}|\leq l_{2}\leq l_{1}+j_{N-2}\,,\dots\,,|l_{N-4}-j_{3}|\leq l_{N-3}\leq l_{N-4}+j_{3}\,.

(B.16)

The only example we really use in our calculation of blocks is the four-valent one, given by

I^{l}_{a_{4},a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}}=\sum_{p_{a}}C^{j_{4}j_{3}l}_{a_{4}a_{3}p_{a}}C^{lj_{2}j_{1}}_{p_{a},a_{2},a_{1}}\,.

(B.17)

For convenience, we can introduce the following tensors that are defined through an exchange of columns in one of the two defining Clebsch-Gordan coefficients as in equations (
B.8a
) and (
B.8b
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}^{l}_{a_{4},a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{p_{a}}C^{j_{4}j_{3}l}_{a_{4},a_{3},p_{a}}C^{j_{2}j_{1}l}_{a_{2},a_{1},p_{a}}=(-1)^{j_{2}-a_{2}}\sqrt{d_{l}d_{j_{1}}^{-1}}I^{l}_{a_{4},a_{3},-a_{2},a_{1}}\,.

(B.18)

We will use this rewriting only in the context where, due to its contraction with the basis, the switch from
\widetilde{I}
to
I
amounts simply to a complex conjugation together with a dimensional factor. This is the case for the matrices
X=x_{0}\sigma^{0}+ix_{j}\sigma^{j}
in equation (
3.13
). One then has

\begin{split}&\sum_{a_{2},b_{2}}\sum_{a_{3},b_{3}}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{4}}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{b_{1},\dots,b_{4}}\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(X)\mathcal{D}^{j_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}(Y)\\
&=d_{l}d_{j_{4}}^{-1}\sum_{a_{2},b_{2}}\sum_{a_{3},b_{3}}I^{l}_{a_{1},\dots,a_{4}}I^{l}_{b_{1},\dots,b_{4}}\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(X)\overline{\mathcal{D}^{j_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}(Y)}\,,\end{split}

(B.19)

where one uses equation (
B.3
). Because in this case also
\operatorname{det}(Y)
is real, the same holds for the analogous contraction involving
\phi^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{a_{3},b_{3}}(Y)
.

Appendix C
Details of the Computation of Higher Integrals

The computation of the scalar four-point block requires the inner product of two basis elements of
\mathcal{H}L^{2}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
as

\innerproduct{\phi^{j_1,m_1}_{a_1,b_1}}{\phi^{j_2,m_2}_{a_2,b_2}}=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\overline{\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(U)}\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(U)=\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}\delta_{j_{1},j_{2}}\delta_{m_{1},m_{2}}\delta_{a_{1},a_{2}}\delta_{b_{1},b_{2}}

(C.1)

We will in the following, hopefully intuitively, call this case the
(1,1)
-integral. Building on that, we will first compute the
(1,2)
-integral and generalise the computation to the case
(1,N-1)
. We then proceed to calculate the
(2,2)
-integral, which is the fundamental integral for the main part of this publication. We do this by using the
(1,2)
-integral and an expansion of the basis elements similar to the expansion of Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices in (
B.4
). The general integral can be straightforwardly derived from there, but is not important for this publication. Note that the computations of these integrals generalise the computation of the inner product in
Calixto_2011
.

C.1
Decomposition of Elements of
\mathbb{D}_{4}

Any element
M\in\text{SU}(2,2)
has to satisfy

M^{\dagger}HM=H\,,\qquad H=\text{diag}\left(-\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}\right)\,,

(C.2)

by definition together with
\text{det}(M)=1
. We can take the following ansatz

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}N_{1}&UN_{2}\\
U^{\dagger}N_{1}&N_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U_{a}&0\\
0&U_{b}\end{pmatrix}

(C.3)

for
U_{a,b}\in\text{U}(2)
,
N_{1,2}
two positive-definite Hermitian matrices and at this point
U
an arbitrary complex
2\times 2
matrix. We justify this
12
12

12

Furthermore, this is a generalisation of decompositions for
\text{SU}(1,1)
(adapted to
\mathbb{D}
) and
\text{SU}(2)
(adapted to
\mathbb{S}^{2})
, see for example equation (
C.5
).
by already knowing at this point that we want to consider a Hilbert space over quotient of
\text{SU}(2,2)
by its maximally compact subgroup
\text{S}(\text{U}(2)\times\text{U}(2))
.
13
13

13

The Lie algebras of
\text{S}(\text{U}(2)\times\text{U}(2))
and
\text{SU}(2)\times\text{SU}(2)\times\text{U}(1)
are isomorphic.
In other words, this is the decomposition adjusted to the specific structure of this symmetric space. It was checked in
Ruehl:1972jy
that this ansatz satisfies the defining
\text{U}(2,2)
conditions (
C.2
) in the case where
U\in\mathbb{D}_{4}
(see definition (
3.1
)) and they furthermore determine for example

N_{1}=\left(\mathbb{1}-UU^{\dagger}\right)^{-1/2}\,,\quad N_{2}=\left(\mathbb{1}-U^{\dagger}U\right)^{-1/2}\,,\quad U_{a}=N_{1}^{-1}A\,,\quad U_{b}=N_{2}^{-1}D\,,

(C.4)

amounting to a polar decomposition of
A
and
D
.

Then, it is convenient to drop the determinant condition
\text{det}(U_{a}U_{b})=1
in the following, i. e. to consider
\text{U}(2,2)/\text{U}(2)^{2}
instead. This allows us to apply the analogue decomposition to the elements in both of the
\text{U}(2)
factors

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta&z\delta\\
-\bar{z}\delta&\delta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{i\beta_{1}}&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{i\beta_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(C.5)

for
\delta=(1-z\overline{z})^{-1/2}
. Note that both of these decompositions lead in turn to decompositions of the integral measure
\operatorname{d}\!\mu(g)

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}\!\mu(g)=\operatorname{d}\!\mu(g)\big|_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\operatorname{d}\!\nu(U_{a})\operatorname{d}\!\nu(U_{b})\,,\qquad\operatorname{d}\!\mu(g)\big|_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}=\mathrm{det}^{-4}\left(\mathbb{1}-UU^{\dagger}\right)\operatorname{d}\!U

(C.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}\!\nu(U_{a,b})=(1+z\overline{z})^{-2}\operatorname{d}\!z\,\beta_{1}\operatorname{d}\!\beta_{1}\,\beta_{2}\operatorname{d}\!\beta_{2}

(C.7)

where
\operatorname{d}\!U
and
\operatorname{d}\!z
are the Lebesgue measures on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
and
\mathbb{C}
, respectively. When considering the weighted Bergman space
\mathcal{H}L^{2}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}(\mathbb{D}_{4})
, the measure on
\mathbb{D}_{4}
gets adjusted to
\left[\operatorname{d}\!U\right]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}
in equation (
3.2
), after which
\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}\left[\operatorname{d}\!U\right]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}1=1
(see e. g.
Calixto_2011
).

Because
\mathbb{D}_{4}
is a symmetric space, we can by definition use transformations of the subgroup to simplify its elements, in this case unitary transformations
U_{a,b}
to diagonalise
U\in\mathbb{D}_{4}
. This means in turn that we can parametrie any
U\in\mathbb{D}_{4}
as
14
14

14

This can be seen as a less restrictive singular value decomposition, allowing for complex
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
.

U=U_{a}\Xi U^{\dagger}_{b}

(C.8)

where
U_{a,b}
are unitary and
\Xi=\text{diag}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
is a complex diagonal matrix with
\xi_{1,2}\in\mathbb{D}
. Because this decomposition is only unique up to a phase,
U_{a,b}
should be considered elements of
\text{U}(2)/\text{U}(1)^{2}\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Note that this means that later integrations over
U_{a,b}
have
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
in equation (
C.5
) set to zero, such that the measure in equation (
C.7
) reduces to the measure of
\mathbb{S}^{2}

\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{a,b})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(1+z\overline{z})^{-2}\operatorname{d}\!z\,.

(C.9)

We proceed to parametrise
\xi_{1,2}\in\mathbb{D}
as
\xi_{1,2}=\rho_{1,2}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1,2}}
in standard polar coordinates. It is now straightforward to check that the weight-function under the decomposition in equation (
C.8
) reduces to

\text{det}^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}\left(\mathbb{1}-UU^{\dagger}\right)=\left((1-\rho_{1}^{2})(1-\rho_{2}^{2})\right)^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Omega(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\,.

(C.10)

Furthermore, because
\mathbb{D}_{4}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
, we can rewrite the Lebesgue measure on the latter as

\operatorname{d}\!U=J(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\operatorname{d}\!\xi_{1}\operatorname{d}\!\xi_{2}\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{a})\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{b})\,,\qquad J(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\rho_{1}^{2}-\rho_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}\,,

(C.11)

where
J(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
is the Jacobian determinant. Altogether, this leads to the parametrization of
\left[\operatorname{d}\!U\right]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}
as

\left[\operatorname{d}\!U\right]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}=J(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\Omega(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\operatorname{d}\!\xi_{1}\operatorname{d}\!\xi_{2}\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{a})\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{b})\,.

(C.12)

C.2
Computation of
(1,2)
-Integral

According to the discussion in the previous section, choosing
U=U_{a}\Xi U_{b}^{\dagger}
gives us for the
(1,2)
-integral

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\phi_{a_1,b_1}^{j_1,m_1}}{\phi_{a_2,b_2}^{j_2,m_2}\phi_{a_3,b_3}^{j_3,m_3}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\overline{\mathrm{det}^{m_{1}}(U)\mathcal{D}_{a_{1},b_{1}}^{j_{1}}(U)}\mathrm{det}^{m_{2}}(U)\mathcal{D}_{a_{2},b_{2}}^{j_{2}}(U)\mathrm{det}^{m_{3}}(U)\mathcal{D}_{a_{3},b_{3}}^{j_{3}}(U)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}c_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!\xi_{1}\operatorname{d}\!\xi_{2}\,J(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\Omega(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\mathrm{det}^{m_{1}}(\overline{\Xi})\mathrm{det}^{m_{2}+m_{3}}(\Xi)\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{c_{1},c_{1}}(\overline{\Xi})\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{c_{2},c_{2}}(\Xi)\mathcal{D}^{j_{3}}_{c_{3},c_{3}}(\Xi)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\qquad\quad\,\times\int_{\mathrm{U}(2)/\mathrm{U}(1)^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{a})\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{a_{1},c_{1}}(\overline{U_{a}})\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},c_{2}}(U_{a})\mathcal{D}^{j_{3}}_{a_{3},c_{3}}(U_{a})

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\qquad\quad\,\times\int_{\mathrm{U}(2)/\mathrm{U}(1)^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{b})\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{b_{1},c_{1}}(U_{b})\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{b_{2},c_{2}}(\overline{U}_{b})\mathcal{D}^{j_{3}}_{b_{3},c_{3}}(\overline{U}_{b})\,,

(C.13)

where we have used that
\mathrm{det}(U_{a,b})=1
(following from equation (
C.5
)) and equation (
B.1
). We consider first the disk integrations. Because

\mathcal{D}_{a,b}^{j}(\Xi)=\delta_{a,b}\xi_{1}^{j+a}\xi_{2}^{j-a}\,,\qquad\mathrm{det}(\Xi)=\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\,,\qquad\xi_{1,2}=\rho_{1,2}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1,2}}\,,

(C.14)

we get for the integrand of the disk integrations

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\rho_{1}^{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+c_{1}+c_{2}+c_{3}+m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}+1}\rho_{2}^{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}-c_{1}-c_{2}-c_{3}+m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}+1}\\
&\times\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}(-j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}-c_{1}+c_{2}+c_{3}-m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})}\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{2}(-j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+c_{1}-c_{2}-c_{3}-m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})}\,.\end{split}

(C.15)

Integration over the angular part gives then

4\pi^{2}\delta_{c_{1},c_{2}+c_{3}}\delta_{k_{1},k_{2}+k_{3}}

(C.16)

where we abbreviated
k_{i}=j_{i}+m_{i}
. When taking into account these Kronecker deltas, the radial part (together with the factor of
4\pi^{2}
from equation (
C.16
)) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{c_{1}}^{k}&\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=4\pi^{2}c_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\rho_{1}\operatorname{d}\!\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\operatorname{d}\!\rho_{2}J(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\Omega(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\rho_{1}^{2(k+c_{1})}\rho_{2}^{2(k-c_{1})}\\
&=2\pi^{2}c_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{d}\!\rho_{1}\,\int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{d}\!\rho_{2}\,\Big\{\rho_{1}^{2(k+c_{1}+2)+1}(1-\rho_{1}^{2})^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}\rho_{2}^{2(k-c_{1})+1}(1-\rho_{2}^{2})^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}\\
&\quad\,+\rho_{1}^{2(k+c_{1})+1}(1-\rho_{1}^{2})^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}\rho_{2}^{2(k-c_{1}+2)+1}(1-\rho_{2}^{2})^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}\\
&\quad\,-2\rho_{1}^{2(k+c_{1}+1)+1}(1-\rho_{1}^{2})^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}\rho_{2}^{2(k-c_{1}+1)+1}(1-\rho_{2}^{2})^{\mathbb{\Delta}-4}\Big\}\,,\end{split}

(C.17)

where we used equations (
C.10
) and (
C.11
). We can now use in every summand the elementary integral

\int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{d}\!x\,x^{2a+1}(1-x^{2})^{b}=\frac{\Gamma(a+1)\Gamma(b+1)}{\Gamma(a+b+2)}\,,

(C.18)

and together with
c_{\mathbb{\Delta}}=\pi^{-4}(\mathbb{\Delta}-1)(\mathbb{\Delta}-2)^{2}(\mathbb{\Delta}-3)
we arrive at

\displaystyle R^{k}_{c_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}^{2}(2\mathbb{\Delta}-5)+k^{2}+\mathbb{\Delta}(k+1)-1}{\pi^{2}(\mathbb{\Delta}-1)}\binom{k+c_{1}+\mathbb{\Delta}-1}{\mathbb{\Delta}-1}^{-1}\binom{k-c_{1}+\mathbb{\Delta}-1}{\mathbb{\Delta}-1}^{-1}\,.

(C.19)

We continue with the spherical integrals in equation (
C.13
). Both integrals are a special case of the SU(2) integrals given in equation (
B.13
). Because of the invariance of
U_{a}
, we can add an extra integration and compensate by the appropriate volume factor, ending up with
15
15

15

This is again up to constant prefactor.

\int_{\mathrm{U}(2)/\mathrm{U}(1)^{2}}\operatorname{d}\!s(U_{a})\overline{\mathcal{D}^{j_{1}}_{a_{1},c_{1}}(U_{a})}\mathcal{D}^{j_{2}}_{a_{2},c_{2}}(U_{a})\mathcal{D}^{j_{3}}_{a_{3},c_{3}}(U_{a})=\frac{\pi}{d_{j_{1}}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{c_{3},c_{2},c_{1}}\,.

(C.20)

The
U_{b}
integral gives the analogue result, if one carefully accounts for the phases after using equation (
B.3
) and (
B.8c
). We can then evaluate the
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
sums in equation (
C.13
), using the orthogonality relation (
B.7
). The remaining
c_{1}
sum can then be evaluated over the result of the radial integral in equation (
C.19
). Grouping together with the numerical prefactors from the
U_{a,b}
-integrals, one obtains the same coefficient as in the inner product (
C.1
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\pi^{2}}{d_{j_{1}}^{2}}\sum\limits_{c_{1}=-j_{1}}^{j_{1}}R^{j_{1}+m_{1}}_{c_{1}}&=\frac{\mathbb{\Delta}-1}{d_{j_{1}}}\binom{m_{1}+\mathbb{\Delta}-2}{\mathbb{\Delta}-2}^{-1}\binom{d_{j_{1}}+m_{1}+\mathbb{\Delta}-2}{\mathbb{\Delta}-2}^{-1}\\
&=(\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\mathbb{\Delta}})^{-2}\,.\end{split}

(C.21)

Putting everything together again, the
(1,2)
-integral evaluates hence to

\innerproduct{\phi_{a_1,b_1}^{j_1,m_1}}{\phi_{a_2,b_2}^{j_2,m_2}\phi_{a_3,b_3}^{j_3,m_3}}=\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{b_{3},b_{2},b_{1}}\delta^{k_{1}}_{k_{2}+k_{3}}\,.

(C.22)

This calculation is easily generalised to the
(1,N-1)
-integral by consequent expansion of products of Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
matrices using (
B.4
) and then collapsing a chain of orthogonality relations (
B.7
), yielding

\innerproduct{\phi_{a_1,b_1}^{j_1,m_1}}{\phi_{a_2,b_2}^{j_2,m_2} \cdots\phi_{a_{N-1},b_{N-1}}^{j_{N-1},m_{N-1}}}=\left(\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}\delta^{k_{1}}_{k_{2}+\dots+k_{N-1}}\sum_{l_{1},\dots,l_{N-3}}I^{l_{1}\dots l_{N-3}}_{a_{N},\cdots,a_{1}}I^{l_{1}\dots l_{N-3}}_{b_{N},\cdots,b_{1}}\,,

(C.23)

where the SU(2) intertwiners are defined in equation (
B.15
).

C.3
Computation of the
(2,2)
- and Higher Integrals

Having computed the
(1,2)
-integral, we can now compute in theory every other integral, because we can expand the product of two basis functions in terms of a single basis function, similarly to the SU(2) case in (
B.4
). We present in the following only the
(2,2)
-integral since that suffices for the purpose of this work. From orthogonality we get

\displaystyle\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(U)\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}(U)=\sum_{p_{a},p_{b}}^{l,n}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{2}\innerproduct{\phi^{l,n}_{p_a,p_b}}{\phi^{j_1,m_1}_{a_1,b_1} \phi^{j_2,m_2}_{a_2,b_2}}\phi^{l,n}_{p_{a},p_{b}}(U)\,

(C.24)

for
U\in\mathbb{D}_{4}
.
Note that this expansion reduces to the SU(2) expansion in equation (
B.4
) for
\det(U)=1
.
We can use now equation (
C.24
) to compute the
(2,2)
-integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\innerproduct{\phi^{j_1,m_1}_{a_1,b_1} \phi^{j_2,m_2}_{a_2,b_2} }{\phi^{j_3,m_3}_{a_3,b_3} \phi^{j_4,m_4}_{a_4,b_4} }\\
&=\sum_{p_{a},p_{b}}^{l,n}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}C^{j_{4}j_{3}l}_{a_{4},a_{3},p_{a}}C^{j_{2}j_{1}l}_{a_{2},a_{1},p_{a}}C^{j_{4}j_{3}l}_{b_{4},b_{3},p_{b}}C^{j_{2}j_{1}l}_{b_{2},b_{1},p_{b}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{3}+k_{4}}\\
&=\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{l,n}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{a_{4},a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{b_{4},b_{3},b_{2},b_{1}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{3}+k_{4}}\\
&=\sum_{l,n}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{3}+k_{4}}\end{split}

(C.25)

where we used the definition of
\widetilde{I}
-tensors in equation (
B.18
) and switched columns according to (
B.8c
). We can also evaluate the
n
-sum at this point, leading to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\innerproduct{\phi^{j_1,m_1}_{a_1,b_1} \phi^{j_2,m_2}_{a_2,b_2}}{\phi^{j_3,m_3}_{a_3,b_3} \phi^{j_4,m_4}_{a_4,b_4}}&=\sum_{l}\left(\mathcal{N}^{l,k_{1}+k_{2}-l}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-2}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}}\widetilde{I}^{l}_{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}}\delta^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{k_{3}+k_{4}}\,.\end{split}

(C.26)

We can check that this is still normalised in the right way for e. g.
\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}=\phi^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{a_{2},b_{2}}=1
. Note that the range of summation for
l
is implicitly determined by the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients.

Finally, note that any of the expressions like e. g. the
(1,N-1)
-integral in equation (
C.23
) are conceptually similar to SU(2) expressions like in equation (
B.14
), which are in the literature also known as Haar projectors (see for example
Makinen:2019rou
;
Martin-Dussaud:2019ypf
). To prove that these integrals are actual projectors and as such idempotent, one uses compactness of SU(2) and runs into problems for non-compact examples like e. g.
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. In our case, however, we are working on
\mathbb{D}_{4}
and because
\int_{\mathbb{D}_{4}}[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}1=1
with
[\operatorname{d}\!U]_{\mathbb{\Delta}}
defined in equation (
3.2
), we can proceed in the same way.

C.4
SU(2,2) Adapted Coefficients

The coefficients we encountered in the computation of vacuum and thermal conformal blocks in sections
3.2
and
4
are SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients and 4-valent intertwiners weighted with general linear combinations of conformal weights. We define

\mathcal{C}_{(3),(2),(1)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}=\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{N}^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{\beta}}{{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\gamma}}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}}\right)\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{N}^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{\beta}}{{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\gamma}}}C^{j_{3}j_{2}j_{1}}_{b_{3},b_{2},b_{1}}\right)\delta^{k_{1}}_{k_{2}+k_{3}}

(C.27)

where we again used the block index notation, e. g.
(1)=(j_{1},m_{1},a_{1},b_{1})
. Similarly

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{(1),(2),(3),(4)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}

(C.28)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(E)}\mathcal{C}_{(1),(2),(E)}^{\alpha\beta\mathbb{\Delta}}\mathcal{C}_{(3),(4),(E)}^{\mathbb{\Delta}\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p_{a},p_{b}}^{l,n}\left(\frac{\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{j_{2},m_{2}}_{\beta}}{\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}}C^{j_{1}j_{2}l}_{a_{1},a_{2},p_{a}}C^{j_{1}j_{2}l}_{b_{1},b_{2},p_{b}}\right)\left(\frac{\mathcal{N}^{j_{3},m_{3}}_{\gamma}\mathcal{N}^{j_{4},m_{4}}_{\delta}}{\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}}C^{j_{3}j_{4}l}_{a_{3},a_{4},p_{a}}C^{j_{3}j_{4}l}_{b_{3},b_{4},p_{b}}\right)\delta^{l+n}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}\delta^{l+n}_{k_{3}+k_{4}}\,.

where
(E)=(l,n,p_{a},p_{b})
is just the tupel we sum over. We define the weighted basis polynomials

\varphi^{(1)}_{\alpha}(U)=\mathcal{N}^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{\alpha}\phi^{j_{1},m_{1}}_{a_{1},b_{1}}(U)

(C.29)

for
\alpha
a general linear combination of conformal weights and which are for
\alpha=\mathbb{\Delta}
normalised with respect to the inner product (
C.1
).
16
16

16

For such normalised basis elements, the coefficient (
C.27
) is just the coefficient appearing in the expansion (
C.24
), i. e.
\varphi^{(1)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\varphi^{(2)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}=\sum_{(E)}\mathcal{C}_{(1),(2),(E)}^{\mathbb{\Delta}\mathbb{\Delta}\mathbb{\Delta}}\varphi^{(E)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}
.
The
(2,2)
- and the
(1,2)
-integrals of the weighted basis elements (
C.29
) are given by

\displaystyle\innerproduct{ \varphi^{(1)}_{\alpha} \varphi^{(2)}_{\beta}}{ \varphi^{(3)}_{\gamma} \varphi^{(4)}_{\delta}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}_{(1),(2),(3),(4)}\,,\qquad\innerproduct{ \varphi^{(1)}_{\alpha} }{\varphi^{(2)}_{\beta} \varphi^{(3)}_{\gamma}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}^{\,-\,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{0,(1),(2),(3)}\,.

(C.30)

where the integrals are always with respect to the weight
\mathbb{\Delta}
. The defining functions in our computations are now defined as a contraction of
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{(1),(2),(3),(4)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
and two weighted basis elements

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{(1),(4)}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\\
\mathbb{\Delta}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg|X_{1},X_{2}\right)\equiv\sum_{(2),(3)}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{(1),(2),(3),(4)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\varphi^{(2)}_{\beta}(X_{1})\varphi^{(3)}_{\gamma}(X_{2})\,,

(C.31)

where
\mathbb{\Delta}
indicates the weight of the defining contraction in
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
. We often leave out the dependence on the conformal weights for readability.

Properties of these weighted coefficients are generally different from the original SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients, for example
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{(1),(2),(3)}=\mathcal{C}^{\beta\alpha\gamma}_{(2),(1),(3)}
. However, one still has
\mathcal{C}_{(1),0,(1)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}=1
. Further special cases that we use in the context of the low-temperature limit of conformal blocks are

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,0,(3),(4)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle=\delta_{(3),0}\delta_{(4),0}\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,(2),(3),0}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\beta}\mathcal{N}^{(3)}_{\gamma}}{\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\mathcal{N}^{(3)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}}\delta_{(2),(3)}\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,(2),(3),(4)}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{\gamma\delta\beta}_{(3),(4),(2)}\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{(1),(2),(3),0}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{N}^{(3)}_{\mathbb{\Delta}}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha\beta\gamma}_{(1),(2),(3)}\,,

(C.32)

where we used equation (
B.9
). Clearly,
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{0,0,0,0}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}=1
.

C.5
Weighted SU(2) Spin-Networks

We found in the main text that the conformal blocks we calculated are naturally series of weighted SU(2) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients. Consequently, we can introduce weighted versions of SU(2) spin-network diagrams. We denote
C^{j_{1}j_{2}l}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}
and
I_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}}^{l}
as
17
17

17

We use a really rudimentary version of the actual standard SU(2) diagrammatic rules given in for example
Makinen:2019rou
;
Martin-Dussaud:2019ypf
. For example, we do not care about orientations of the diagrams and rely more on context. Note that according to equation (
B.19
), for the conformal blocks the left side of the
I^{l}
equation should actually say
\sqrt{d_{l}d_{j_{4}}^{-1}}I_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}}^{l}
, we leave these additional factors out for simplicity.

\displaystyle C^{j_{1}j_{2}l}_{a_{1},a_{2},c}=\hbox to72.03pt{\vbox to46.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.82178pt\lower-23.26236pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(C.33)

The lines are only labelled by the parameters
j_{i}
that label the SU(2) representation, not the magnetic numbers
a_{i},b_{i}
. Usually, the lines are contracted and hence the magnetic numbers summed over anyways. One can now adjust the diagrammatics (
C.33
) by attaching a factor of
\mathcal{N}^{j,m}_{\delta}
to a line with label
j
, (where
\delta
is a stand-in for a general combination of conformal weights). Furthermore, lines are labelled with
\delta
instead, where
\delta
denotes the whole tupel
(\delta,j,m,a,n)
and the block-index
(j,m,a,b)
is summed over when the lines are contracted. From the expansion in equation (
C.24
) we find that the spin-coupled
l
-lines in (
C.33
) are weighted with
(\mathcal{N}^{l,n}_{\mathbb{\Delta}})^{-1}
. Moreover, from the same expansion we find that each vertex is labelled with the appropriate Kronecker delta: for example, the vertex in the left diagram in (
C.33
) gets weighted with
\delta^{l+n}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}
for
k_{i}=j_{i}+m_{i}
, the vertices in the right diagram with
\delta^{l+n}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}
and
\delta^{l+n}_{k_{3}+k_{4}}
. In the thermal diagrams (
4.14
) and (
4.19
), the thermal loops together with the vertical lines of the left-most
X
-matrix are weighted with
q^{k_{i}}
.

We have decided to label the diagrams for comb and thermal
n
-point blocks, see for example (
3.37
) and (
4.19
) with the respective conformal weights, which in theory allows to read off the explicit expressions from just the diagrams. Keeping the SU(2) spin notation as in the diagrams (
C.33
) would emphasise the contractions in the magnetic numbers and view the block as a weighted series of such a SU(2) amplitude (with the caveat that involved Wigner-
\mathcal{D}
are more general than SU(2)). This has the advantage that there are very efficient numerical packages for the evaluation SU(2) spin-networks, we commented on this in section
5
.

Appendix D
Computation of Projection Channel Blocks in Two Dimensions

The aim of this section is to pedagogically illustrate how the oscillator method is used for thermal blocks in the simpler case of two dimensions to get a better understanding of the method. The construction was reviewed for comb blocks on the two-dimensional plane in
Ammon:2024axd
, the idea being to introduce the weighted Bergman space
\mathcal{H}L^{2}_{\mathbb{h}}(\mathbb{D})
. This is a natural irreducible representation of one sector of the global conformal algebra
\mathfrak{su}(1,1)
because
\mathbb{D}\cong\text{SU}(1,1)/\text{U}(1)
. Details on the basics of the formalism in two dimensions (like the explicit form of the
\mathfrak{su}(1,1)
generators and the definition of the inner product) can be found in
Ammon:2024axd
.

To obtain thermal blocks, we map the (punctured) plane to a cylinder and then to a torus using conformal transformations, which does not change the parametrization of the
\mathfrak{L}_{n}
generators, but of the adjoint action on primaries. Let us demonstrate this procedure by considering the matrix element
\Omega(z;u_{1},\bar{u}_{2})=\matrixelement{u_1}{\mathcal{O}_h(z)}{\bar{u}_2}
on the plane, see
Ammon:2024axd
. Mapping the plane to the cylinder, we introduce a new coordinate via
z=\mathrm{e}^{w}
and taking the transformation behaviour of the primary into account, the matrix element on the cylinder reads

\Omega(w;u_{1},\bar{u}_{2})=\mathrm{e}^{hw}(\mathrm{e}^{w}-\bar{u}_{2})^{\mathbb{h}_{1}-\mathbb{h}_{2}-h}(1-\mathrm{e}^{w}u_{1})^{\mathbb{h}_{2}-\mathbb{h}_{1}-h}(1-u_{1}\bar{u}_{2})^{h-\mathbb{h}_{1}-\mathbb{h}_{2}}\,.

(D.1)

We could derive the same result by transforming
\mathfrak{L}^{(z)}_{n}
to
\mathfrak{L}^{(w)}_{n}
and solving the corresponding oscillator equation

\displaystyle 0=\left(\mathfrak{L}_{n}^{(u_{1})}-\bar{\mathfrak{L}}_{-n}^{(\bar{u}_{2})}+\mathcal{L}_{n}^{(w)}\right)\;\Omega(w;u_{1},\bar{u}_{2})\,.

(D.2)

The one-point correlation function on the torus for
q=\mathrm{e}^{2\pi i\tau}
can be defined as

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{O}}=(q\bar{q})^{-\frac{c}{24}}\text{Tr}\left(q^{L_{0}}q^{\bar{L}_{0}}\mathcal{O}\right)\,,

(D.3)

where we will in the following consider only the holomorphic sector. Inserting then a projection operator into the one-point function and expressing it through an oscillator integral, we arrive at an expression for the one-point block of the holomorphic sector involving the matrix element in equation (
D.1
)

\begin{split}G^{(1)}(q)=q^{-\frac{c}{24}}\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbb{h}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{h}}\mathcal{O}_{h}(w)q^{L_{0}}\right)=q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}[\operatorname{d}\!u]\,\Omega(w;u,q\bar{u})\,,\end{split}

(D.4)

in terms of the cylinder matrix element. We have used that the operator
q^{\bar{\mathfrak{L}}_{0}(\bar{u})}
acting on the anti-holomorphic oscillator variable generates a dilation (this is a special case of the four-dimensional calculation in equation (
4.1
)). This result in terms of oscillator diagrams corresponds to the following intuitive gluing rule for the torus one-point block:

\displaystyle G^{(1)}(q)\quad=\quad\hbox to118.28pt{\vbox to68.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-64.08476pt\lower-34.43292pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}}{{{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{67.41777pt}{3.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 196.85 0 M 238.36 0 C 238.36 22.93 219.78 41.51 196.85 41.51 C 173.92 41.51 155.34 22.93 155.34 0 C 155.34 -22.93 173.92 -41.51 196.85 -41.51 C 219.78 -41.51 238.36 -22.93 238.36 0 Z M 196.85 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}}{{{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{174.07974pt}{-1.58018pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{67}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(D.5)

The actual computations proceeds in the ordinary fashion, that is, one expands
\Omega
in monomials and uses the orthogonality relation
(u^{n},u^{m})=\frac{n!}{(2\mathbb{h})_{n}}\delta_{n,m}
to arrive at the power series in
q

\displaystyle q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}[\operatorname{d}\!u]\Omega(w;u,q\bar{u})

\displaystyle=q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}}q^{k_{1}+k_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{-k_{1}w}\mathrm{e}^{k_{2}w}\binom{-h}{k_{1}}\binom{-h}{k_{2}}\binom{h-2\mathbb{h}}{k_{3}}\int_{\mathbb{D}}[d^{2}u]\bar{u}^{k_{1}+k_{3}}u^{k_{2}+k_{3}}

\displaystyle=q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{3}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(h)_{k_{2}}}{k_{2}!}\frac{(h)_{k_{2}}(2\mathbb{h}-h)_{k_{3}}(k_{2}+k_{3})!}{(2\mathbb{h})_{k_{2}+k_{3}}}\frac{q^{k_{2}}}{k_{2}!}\frac{q^{k_{3}}}{k_{3}!}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}}{(1-q)^{h}}{}_{2}F_{1}(1-h,2\mathbb{h}-h,2\mathbb{h},q)\,,

(D.6)

where in the last step several Pochhammer identities and manipulations of the sums were applied. One can verify that this result – as is implied by the construction – fulfils the two-dimensional Casimir equation

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\mathbb{h}}+\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(w)}\right)\mathcal{G}^{(1)}(q)=\left(Q_{\mathbb{h}}-h(h-1)\right)\mathcal{G}^{(1)}(q)=0

(D.7)

with the differential operator
Q_{\mathbb{h}}
given by

\displaystyle Q_{\mathbb{h}}=q(1-q)^{2}\partial_{q}^{2}-2q(1-q)\partial_{q}-\mathbb{h}(\mathbb{h}-1)q^{-1}(1-q)^{2}

(D.8)

and using that
C_{2}^{(w_{1})}\mathcal{O}_{h_{1}}(w_{1})=-h(h-1)\mathcal{O}_{h_{1}}(w_{1})
. This can be achieved (starting for example from the second to last line in (
D
)) by evaluating the derivatives in
q
and performing careful index shifts in
k_{2}
and
k_{3}
to account for the different orders in
q
due to the various prefactors. Equation (
D.7
) can be generalised to the
n
-point case using the multi-point Casimir operator
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})}

\displaystyle\left(Q_{\mathbb{h}}+\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})}\right)\mathcal{G}^{(n)}(q,w_{1},\dots,w_{n})=0\,,

(D.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}^{(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})}=\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\mathfrak{L}^{(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})}_{1}\,,\,\mathfrak{L}^{(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})}_{-1}\bigg\}-\left(\mathfrak{L}^{(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})}_{0}\right)^{2}\,.

(D.10)

Note how the one-point block turns out to be independent of
w
as to be expected from its
\mathfrak{u}(1)
-symmetry. One recovers in the
h=0
limit the zero-point block

\textnormal{Tr}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{h}}q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right)=q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}q^{n}=\frac{q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}}{1-q}\,,

(D.11)

i. e. the character of the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
highest-weight representation. The series expansion of the one-point block in equation (
D
) can be expressed as

G^{(1)}(q)=q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}q^{n}\frac{\tau_{n,n}(\mathbb{h},h,\mathbb{h})}{n!(2\mathbb{h})_{n}}

(D.12)

where we introduced the
\tau
-coefficients

\tau_{k,l}(\mathbb{h}_{1},h,\mathbb{h}_{2})=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\min(k,l)}\frac{(\mathbb{h}_{1}+\mathbb{h}_{2}-h)_{m}(\mathbb{h}_{1}-\mathbb{h}_{2}+h)_{k-m}(\mathbb{h}_{2}-\mathbb{h}_{1}+h)_{l-m}}{m!(k-m)!(l-m)!}\,,

(D.13)

already encountered in the five-point block on the plane
Ammon:2024axd
.

In essentially the same way, one obtains the
n
-point projection channel block

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,w_{1},\dots,w_{n})

\displaystyle=q^{\mathbb{h}-\frac{c}{24}}\int_{\mathbb{D}^{n}}[\operatorname{d}\!u]\,\Omega(w_{1},u_{1},\bar{u}_{2})\Omega(w_{2},u_{2},\bar{u}_{3})\cdots\Omega(w_{n},u_{n},q\bar{u}_{1})

(D.14)

by gluing multiple
\Omega
-wavefunctions. Diagrammatically, this corresponds to the same diagram we used for the four-dimensional case in equation (
2.8
). Note that only the last matrix element carries the dependence on the modular parameter
q
while the
(n-1)
other wavefunctions are the standard matrix elements on the cylinder. Expanding each wavefunction and using the inner product in the usual way, one obtains

\displaystyle G^{(n)}_{\text{Proj.}}(q,w_{1},\dots,w_{n})=q^{\mathbb{h}_{1}-\frac{c}{24}}\sum_{s_{1},\dots s_{n}=0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i,i+1}^{-\mathbb{h}_{i+1}}x_{i,i+1}^{-s_{i+1}}\frac{\tau_{s_{i},s_{i+1}}(\mathbb{h}_{i},h_{i},\mathbb{h}_{i+1})}{s_{i}!(2\mathbb{h}_{i})_{s_{i}}}q^{s_{1}}\,,

(D.15)

with
x_{ij}=\mathrm{e}^{w_{i}-w_{j}}
. Note that this can be brought into the form given in
Alkalaev:2022kal
by inserting
18
18

18

That their
\tau
-coefficients match ours in equation (
D.13
) can be checked by calculating their respective (exponential) generating functions.

\frac{x_{n,1}^{\mathbb{h}_{1}}}{x_{n,1}^{\mathbb{h}_{1}}}=\frac{\prod^{n-1}_{k=1}x^{\mathbb{h}_{1}}_{k+1,k}}{\prod^{n-1}_{k=1}x^{\mathbb{h}_{1}}_{k+1,k}}\,,\qquad\frac{x_{n,1}^{s_{1}}}{x_{n,1}^{s_{1}}}=\frac{\prod^{n-1}_{k=1}x^{s_{1}}_{k+1,k}}{\prod^{n-1}_{k=1}x^{s_{1}}_{k+1,k}}\,,

(D.16)

using the denominator terms to rewrite the first
n-1
terms of
\prod^{n}_{i=1}x^{-\mathbb{h}_{i+1}}_{i,i+1}x^{-s_{i+1}}_{i,i+1}
and cancel the numerator terms with the
n
-th term through
x_{n,1}^{\mathbb{h}_{1}}x_{n,1}^{\mathbb{h}_{1}}=1
and
x_{n,1}^{s_{1}}x_{n,1}^{s_{1}}=1
, finally defining
x_{1}=q/(x_{2}\cdots x_{n})
. In the low-temperature limit
q\to 0
, this thermal block reduces to the comb-channel
(n+2)
-block on the plane with
z_{1}\to\infty
and
z_{n+2}=0
. Because the leading order in
q\to 0
sets
s_{1}=0
in equation (
D.15
), we find that the resulting comb channel block is very similar, with the difference of being dependent on the coefficients
\tau_{0,s_{2}}
and
\tau_{s_{n},0}
. By using that the
\tau
-coefficients in equation (
D.13
) reduce as
\tau_{0,l}=(\mathbb{h}_{2}+h-\mathbb{h}_{1})_{l}
and
\tau_{k,0}=(\mathbb{h}_{1}+h-\mathbb{h}_{2})_{k}
, we see that the
q\to 0
limit for
n=2
is just the usual expression for the four-point block.
